Follow This ‘Map of Quietude’ to the Most Silent Trails in Sweden

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Taking in one of the many gorgeous views along the quiet Swedish coast (Photo: Andrea Bussell)

The invitation piqued my interest as soon as it landed in my inbox: “A beachfront cabin where you can stay for free,” it read, describing a remote spot on the Baltic coast in southwest Sweden. “The only condition? You have to stay quiet.” Surrounded by apple orchards, with the sea just out the door, this idyllic place would be available to a few lucky travelers—as long as we maintained an air of “quietude” during our respective stays.

Outfitted with sound-measuring technology to monitor noise levels inside, the unique setup of the Stay Quiet Cabin is part of a broader initiative by Visit Skåne (pronounced skaw-nuh) to combat one of Europe’s most significant environmental health issues: noise pollution. According to the European Commission, prolonged exposure to loud, chronic noise is the second largest cause of health problems contributing to cardiovascular disease, sleep disturbances, and stress-related health concerns. Spending time in naturally quiet environments can help relieve those symptoms.

Skåne is a region renowned for its restorative pastoral and seaside landscapes, unique rural culture, farm-to-table restaurants, and heritage traditions. Within it, The Map of Quietude—a list of 26 places mapped by a professional sound engineer—leads to many spots along Skåne’s 1,000+ miles of hiking trails. Each spot’s silence has been measured to ensure distinctly healing experiences.

I found that pausing in them to take in the stillness and beauty added something beyond a typical travel or hiking experience. It gave me insight into the true frequency of a place and what it offers: peace, an immersion in rejuvenating slowness, and awe. After my days in silence on the coast, I’ve rounded up some best trails where you can easily experience these locations of quietude, while also taking in interesting communities, historic sites, and some of Scandinavia’s most beautiful wildernesses. Sweden’s nature-centric way of life includes the right of public access, or allemansrätten, which allows hiking or camping almost anywhere in the countryside with the expectation that you will respect the land.

Distance: 7.5 miles Part of the Öresund Way, this loop is worth a detour from inland Skåne to discover a car-free island where people come to bike and hike. You can also get some of the region’s best cardamom buns at Hven Durum, a family-run bakery and mill.

A quick ferry will bring you to this tiny slip of seaside heaven. Walk along the coastline and through a species-rich nature reserve for sweeping views of the Öre Sound and Copenhagen skyline. Ven’s southern headland, södra udde, is a nature reserve and designated place of quietude. See an active, mid-20th-century lighthouse nearby. Or, check out the site of 16th-century Danish astronomer Tycho Brahe’s observatory.

Distance: 7.4 Miles (bonus summit extends to 11.4 miles)

Starting at the edge of the Kulla peninsula in northwest Skåne, you’ll trace roughly seven and a half miles of mostly flat shoreline south from the small fishing village Mölle. One of Sweden’s first seaside resorts at the turn of the 19th century, city dwellers came here for their nature fix. Back then, Mölle was steeped in scandal when, after long hikes, men and women began swimming together in the sea nearby, something that was unthinkable at the time.

The route explores nature reserves and gnarled pine forests and winds past beaches and historic towns. You’ll even pass remains of Kulla fälad, a vast historic pasture that served as a shared grazing land for livestock for centuries. In some places, ancient drystone walls are still visible. You’ll end in Höganäs, a globally renowned center of stoneware and ceramics production. It’s also a great place to explore a burgeoning creative community. Stop by The Hub Höganäs to experience local life, where artists sell heritage-steeped crafts, and locals gather for events. If you’re looking for a great place to eat, try Hubben, a local spot run by friendly Swedes.

To visit a place of Quietude, start in the spectacular Kullaberg Nature Reserve. From there, head to Håkull, the region’s highest peak. The summit offers panoramic views of the sea and Danish coastline. It will add about four miles to your trek.

Distance: 117 miles For over 100 miles, the Österlen Way traverses the southeast corner of Skåne. This is Sweden’s famed culinary region. It’s also where my Stay Quiet Cabin was located. Starting in the medieval town of Ystad, the Österlen Way follows the Baltic Sea coast. It passes through nature reserves, national parks, and fishing villages, with inland stretches through ancient forest.

Among other fascinating stops, you’ll pass the hilltop stone circle, Ales Stenar. This is often referred to as “Sweden’s Stonehenge” for its similarly mysterious origins. The trail has two spots of Quietude. The first is Meditationsplatsen, a circular enclosure where people come to meditate. There’s also Gislövshammar Quarry, a 19th-century limestone quarry on the coast of its namesake fishing village. This area is known for its many orchards, farms, and excellent independent shops and bakeries.

Distance: 113 miles This breathtaking route hugs the wave-swept coastline of the Öresund Strait. From north to south, it passes through historic fishing villages and dramatic landscapes. It combines previously mentioned trails and quiet spots (Håkull, Mölle to Höganäs, and Ven Island) with two additional places of Quietude. There are the historic open-air cold bathing houses Pålsjöbaden and Hilleshögs dalar valley.

Hikers pass beaches, meadows, dunes and cliffs, and enter pine forests. The trail varies in difficulty before finishing with a loop around the Falsterbonäset Peninsula. Stone Age graves, medieval castle ruins, and views of the Danish coast keep things interesting. The final legs of the trail provide ample opportunities for birdwatching. It enters nature reserves that are internationally renowned habitats for many of Northern Europe’s migratory birds.

Distance: 994 miles At just under 1,000 miles (1,600 km), the region’s Skåneleden trail is a long-distance route that traverses a vast amount of the countryside’s changeable terrain. You’ll encounter rocky coast, verdant forest, national parks, and nature reserves.  There are also historic sites like castles and stone circles.

The Skåneleden Trail is divided into seven sub-trails by geographic area. There are 148 sections (encompassing all the previously mentioned routes). Many individual sections make for great day hikes. Or, combine them for longer treks over multiple days or weeks. Several sections are near train or bus stations, making transportation easy. Basic campsites with wind shelters and bathrooms are available along the way for those seeking a longer adventure.

**You can hear 60-minute field recordings of birdsong, waves, and wind from each of the places of quietude mentioned above on Visit Skåne’s website or Spotify.

Nanofat Is Dermatologists’ Best-Kept Secret for Younger, Fresher Skin

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

It’s not an injectable filler, and it’s not fat transfer. It’s a secret third thing you’ll want to try.

The last few years have been unkind to our facial fat. We sliced out our buccal pads to create sunken-in, razor-sharp cheekbones. We zapped our skin with thermal devices that accidentally melted fat away in pursuit of boosting collagen. We injected our stomachs with fat-loss GLP-1 medications, leaving our faces looking hollow. Droopy. Structurally unsound. As it turns out, facial fat is critical infrastructure—not just for looking younger, but for your skin's overall health and quality. And while you can’t choose where you lose or gain fat, you can choose to have a doctor siphon it, remix it, and reinject it.

“Adipose tissue is a rich source of growth factors and progenitor cells, and re-injecting fat is probably one of the best ways to regenerate the full potential of healthy skin I've seen in the last decades,” says Patricia Ogilvie, a Munich-based dermatologist and member of the Dior Reverse Aging Board. “There are trends that are not really worth being a trend. I think the restaging of the importance of adipose tissue is very justified.”

You’re probably familiar with fat transfer, a procedure that’s been around for decades. In simple terms, fat transfer takes adipose tissue from one area of your body, like your flanks, liposuctions it, and moves it to another area, like your breasts or cheekbones. “With traditional fat transfer, it’s about restoring volume. And because it’s your own living tissue, it can integrate beautifully and give you very natural-looking results,” says dermatologist Sheerene Idriss, medical director and founder of Idriss Dermatology in New York City. “But what’s interesting is that fat isn’t just fat. It also contains regenerative cells, including adipose-derived stem cells, and growth factors that may help improve the surrounding tissue over time. Nanofat is where it gets really exciting.”

Nanofat is part of a new class of regenerative aesthetics offered by dermatologists and plastic surgeons, treatments that don’t just make you look better, but rather address the signs of aging from the inside out by stimulating collagen or repairing tissue. Imagine taking that same fat from a traditional fat transfer and processing it into a fine, milky liquid rich in your own stem cells and growth factors, then injecting or microneedling it back into your face—that’s nanofat.

“Instead of using it to restore volume, you’re using it to potentially improve skin quality itself,” says Idriss. “These cells and growth factors communicate with surrounding tissue and can stimulate processes involved in collagen production, tissue repair, and blood vessel formation. So you’re not just trying to fill a wrinkle. You’re trying to improve the skin itself—its thickness, resilience, and texture—so that wrinkle potentially becomes less noticeable.”

The best areas for nanofat injections include under the eye, a notoriously risky area for hyaluronic acid fillers, and the cheekbones. “The ideal patient for nanofat is someone who's starting to notice thinner, crepier skin, fine lines, or changes in skin texture, especially around the eyes, neck, or chest, and wants to improve skin quality rather than add volume,” adds Idriss, who both has had the treatment done and offers it in her practice. Plus, Ogilvie notes, nanofat doesn’t attract water the way fillers do, so it won’t cause under-eye puffiness (a common post-filler complaint). But because nanofat doesn’t restore volume as much as it reinvigorates your skin, you can inject it almost anywhere you’re noticing signs of aging.

“We do it under tumescent local anesthesia, so you’re awake the entire time, and recovery is usually pretty quick,” says Idriss. “I personally had nanofat and fat transfer in June and was back at work the next day. The biggest downside is some soreness at the fat extraction site, which usually lasts a few days, and you’ll want to avoid strenuous exercise for about a week. Because there’s no general anesthesia, you’re also avoiding the grogginess and recovery associated with being put under.”

Like any cosmetic treatment, it has risks and downsides. Experts note that nanofat isn’t the right treatment for anyone looking to restore significant volume loss or seeking instant results. “Improvement in skin quality can vary from person to person, and it takes months to see the full results,” Idriss adds. “There are rare but serious risks, including infection, vascular complications, and even vision loss when injecting certain areas of the face.” But patients who have tried the procedure say the recovery is quick and easy, with results compounding over weeks, though they ultimately fade away faster than traditional injectables.

Nanofat injections sit somewhere between microfat and hyaluronic acid fillers. Microfat can be lumpy and unpredictable when injected, sometimes leading to lopsided results. Plus, in any fat transfer, only a small amount of fat survives. Fillers, on the other hand, offer long-lasting precision and volume restoration, but patients are tired of looking overfilled and unnaturally plump. Nanofat helps your skin look naturally healthier, fresher, and firmer, at least for a while. After all, it’s made from you.

“I look at this as something you can do periodically throughout your life, but what you do in between treatments is just as important. And that’s why I don’t believe in demonizing any one treatment, including filler,” says Idriss. “When used appropriately, for the right indication, these treatments can all work beautifully together. It’s about knowing what you need, when you need it, and when enough is enough.”

Jenna Dewan Faced Criticism, a Partner Suspension, and a Shock <i><em>Dancing with the Stars </em></i>Exit. She’s Still Proud.

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

In an exclusive interview with Women’s Health, the actor reflects on her time in the ballroom.

Dancing with the Stars fans let out a collective gasp Tuesday night. Jenna Dewan and her partner, Val Chmerkovskiy, had earned one of the night’s top scores: 28 out of 30. They also won their dance-off against Tatyana Ali and Jan Ravnik, tying for second on the leaderboard with Amber Glenn and Pasha Pashkov. Then host Alfonso Ribeiro called Dewan’s name, eliminating her from competition.

“I had a great time, and I loved getting to share that with the world,” she said, moments after being eliminated. “All love.”

Not even 48 hours later, Jenna sat down with Women’s Health to share her story.

Jenna Dewan: Overall, it’s really been the saying, “Two things can be true at once.” There's so many different emotions, and it's kind of hard to even put into words because obviously doing a show like this is such an enormous undertaking, and it's physically, emotionally, mentally, spiritually intense. And so, when that's just finished, it's like a light switch goes off, and then you almost don't even know where you are.

I feel all kinds of things. I feel mostly proud. I feel really proud of myself, and I feel proud of the last dance we gave on the ballroom floor, and I feel obviously sad that it's over, that I don't get to give any more dances out there and create any more special pieces. So I think it's really both. I feel really proud of us, and I feel fulfilled, and I feel filled up, and I also feel sad that the journey of creating dances is over.

JD: When you are going through the show, it feels like any week could be you. The show is not necessarily a show about dance. It's not necessarily a competition about the best dancer. It's not about the best dance you give. It's so many other elements that go into it, which is great on a reality show front-end. It makes people tune in for all different kinds of reasons, but in doing that, you never know how it's going to go.

Once it started whittling down, it was the two of us. You kind of come to a place of like, “Okay, this could be our moment.” And I had a feeling—it was a tough week as far as some of the online noise, I would say, that goes with doing the show. So it definitely wasn't out of my mind that it could be a possibility.

Even before Jenna stepped onto the DWTS dance floor, viewers debated whether she belonged in the competition. How much prior dance experience a celebrity contestant can have—while still being considered a fair competitor—has long been a point of debate among viewers.

Jenna started her career as a dancer, where she toured alongside legendary performers, including Janet Jackson (she appeared in two music videos of the singer’s). In 2006, she landed her breakout role in Step Up, in which she played a classically trained ballerina. She was the host for television shows World of Dance and Flirty Dancing as well as a judge on Come Dance with Me. Many fans of DWTS cited Jenna’s impressive resume as an unfair advantage. “How is Jenna Dewan part of this? She was a professional dancer. Hello! So unfair already,” one X user wrote on the platform after the official season 35 cast announcement.

It should be noted, however, Jenna is not the first celebrity to join the show with previous dance experience—and it’s safe to say she’ll be far from the last. Stars like Heather Morris, Jordan Fisher, and Charli D’Amelio have all undergone criticism for joining the show in the past, despite having a substantial leg up among fellow contestants. Even Alfonso, the show’s current host and season 19 winner, had prior dance experience.

JD: I will say, through the history of watching the show and what I know about the show, is I've seen there be an issue with people with dance backgrounds, and that obviously there's a lot of opinions on whether we should even have people compete on the show that have dance backgrounds.

I definitely asked quite a few times, “Are you sure you guys want me on the show?” But the incredible people who do the show were really confident in expressing to me that this show had changed a lot, and that it’s not Dancing with the Stars Who Have Never Danced. They wanted a very dynamic cast with all different levels and strengths they bring to the ballroom. I knew it would be an uphill battle for me for sure, just from the patterns of the show and the loyalists of the show and how they feel. But ultimately, the gift of doing something just for myself was really intoxicating, and it won over any sort of doubts or hesitations I had.

JD: The hardest part of the show, I would say, is it really teaches you that joy and difficulty can exist together. There were so many highs of this journey, and then so many moments of frustration and negative noise and criticism that is honestly sad, because it tries to suck out the joy from the experience.

Even for myself with a dance background, it is vulnerable to put yourself out there in the ballroom every Tuesday and be like, “Hey guys, watch my dance and tell me what you think.” And then you have the whole other element of this social, online, noisy world that is almost like they're looking to tear people down, and I felt that that was an unfortunate shade to this show that didn't need to happen.

JD: Part of the journey of the show is interesting because for the first good couple weeks, you rarely see any of the other contestants, because you are really in your own rehearsal space, and you're just trying to get the dances down as fast as you can with your partner. I think it was rare seeing people in the hallways and connecting with some of the contestants. But yeah, we definitely would talk.

I think at one point Connor [Wood] made me laugh so hard because he said he had seen some of the online social negativity, and he said, “Everyone needs to go back to journaling.” I laughed so hard, because he is exactly right. At some point, we all thought it was okay to just get online and to be mean for no reason. We are real human beings that see this and feel this, and I think that that gets forgotten.

When we did the team dance, that was when we really were able to start bonding as the contestants, because we were finally together working towards one dance and in a room together. I absolutely love them, and we were all definitely talking about this and sharing our experiences with this, and how most of us at that point, I think, had taken it off our phone or turned off social media.

JD: Naturally, because of my dance background, there were really high expectations that I put on myself, and that the world was putting on me. I didn't even realize it was happening, but it was creating this feeling that I needed to let go of perfection and kind of just get comfortable being seen and just being there. I didn't need to prove anything.

I think I just realized how much of my life I spent wanting to get things right. And this experience really challenged me in that way, because what I realized is, you just can't grow if you're constantly worried about being perfect. I had to learn to be a little uncomfortable, to be critiqued again, to make some mistakes, to be vulnerable, and just let people see me in the middle of figuring something out. There's actually a lot of freedom in that, because I didn't have ballroom dance down perfectly by the time I got out there.

JD: I thought that every dance we did. I thought that during the week two jive, and it was intriguing to me that [the judges] were so critical of certain dances. However, when I look back, there was a lot of growth for me.

There was a whole element of this that was sort of discovering my strength in a new way because I have had three children, I've navigated huge life transitions, I've built a career, a family, a life, And now, I'm stepping into this show where I'm pushing myself physically, emotionally, creatively in a way that I haven't in years. So I think I just surprised myself with how strong I am, and not just physically, but emotionally.

Jenna also faced an unexpected complication when her partner, Val, was suspended for one episode of the show following a social media post he made about DWTS judge Carrie Ann Inaba. After Carrie Ann criticized his wife, fellow pro dancer Jenna Johnson, for including what she considered an illegal lift in her routine with Harry Shum Jr., Val posted, “You almost saw Fresh Prince enter the ballroom last night.” The now-deleted post was widely interpreted as a reference to the 2022 Academy Awards, when Will Smith slapped Chris Rock after making a joke about his wife, Jada Pinkett Smith.

Carrie Ann appeared to interpret the post as a threat. She shared a screenshot of a Page Six story about it on Instagram, writing, “So this is what we are doing now?”

On Oct. 17, DWTS production company BBC Studios said Val would not participate in the Oct. 20 episode. Jenna danced with pro Alan Bersten in his place.

JD: I said this, and I really mean this, that I did not foresee having as many challenges as I did that were thrown at me during my Dancing with the Stars journey. However, the challenges of it gave me an opportunity to walk my talk, and I always think that when that happens, you can go back to your true north, to what’s your intention for doing something. How do you want to show up as a human when you’re experiencing challenges? And I kept going back to my kids. When the mid-season suspension happened, and all these things were going on, I thought, “I have a real opportunity here. I can be annoyed and bitter and upset about this, or I can show up with grace and love and joy and focus on the moment.”

That particular week was about my mom and my dedication to her. And she was in town, thrilled to have this dance with me, and thrilled to experience this moment. And I thought that this is something she's going to remember. We're all going to remember my family forever. Let's focus on that, and let's not worry about all of this that's going on.

JD: I’ve seen that, and I feel like with just life in general, even outside of Dancing with the Stars, that is absolutely something that most females have experienced. I think there's been a great change in our world, but I think for years upon years upon years, women have had a fight against misogyny and inherent bias. Women at times in life are just held to a different standard than men, and I think that that is a real reflection.

We're expected to be exceptional, but also humble and confident, but not too confident, skilled, but not struggling enough to earn praise. There's all this pressure on women. It feels like sometimes if a woman is strong or capable, the expectations become that much higher. I think people are so busy expecting women to be extraordinary that we forget to celebrate them for being extraordinary.

JD: It's never too late for a new chapter in life. And I think if there was like, one thing I wanted people to take away from my whole journey, it’s that we're never finished becoming who we are, and it's never too late to choose your own passion in life and your own joy in life. There isn't some age where you stop growing and dreaming and trying new things.

Alyssa Cruz is the social media editor at Women’s Health, where she drives social strategy and helps shape the brand’s voice across platforms. Prior to WH, she worked at CBS News and palabra. Originally from Toledo, she is a proud alumna of Ohio University, where she received two bachelor’s degrees in journalism and Spanish. When she is not creating social content, you can find her trying different restaurants in New York City or planning her next trip.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/regulatory/trump-administration-delays-enforcement-medicaid-work-requirements-six-months" hreflang="en">Trump administration delays enforcement of Medicaid work requirements for six months</a>

📅 2026-11-13  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
Trump administration delays enforcement of Medicaid work requirements for six months" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

The Trump administration is giving states an optional six-month grace period for enforcing new Medicaid work requirements, which were due to go into effect January 1.

The Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS) announced Friday an optional "hold harmless" period through June 30, 2027, to give states a transition period to further modernize systems, improve processes and streamline the applicant and beneficiary experience.

During the "hold harmless" period, states will still assess whether Medicaid applicants and beneficiaries meet the work requirements or qualify for an exclusion, but otherwise eligible people will not lose or be denied coverage solely because they fail to meet the requirement, CMS said.

CMS says the transition is intended to give states time to improve eligibility systems, automate verification, train workers and improve the beneficiary experience.

"States have made real progress, and they’ve been clear with us about what it takes to get this right for beneficiaries—at scale and for the long term," Dan Brillman, Deputy Administrator and Director of the Center for Medicaid and CHIP Services, said in a statement. "If the state can use data to show that someone is working or qualifies for an exclusion, that person should not have to track down paperwork to prove it. And when someone needs support, they should know exactly what to do, when to do it, and where to go. That’s how we build a Medicaid program that works better for people, connects them to work, education, and training opportunities, and supports paths to prosperity."

During the six-month enforcement pause, CMS encourages states to use existing data to automatically verify qualifying activities and exemptions before requesting additional information from beneficiaries.

States are also encouraged to simplify notices and make the process easy to navigate so beneficiaries understand what the state has already verified, what information is still needed and how to respond or get help. States should also test eligibility systems end-to-end, particularly how the systems handle missing, outdated or conflicting information, to resolve operational issues and minimize inappropriate coverage disruptions.

CMS also called on states to coordinate with community partners to connect applicants and beneficiaries who need assistance with opportunities for employment, education, job training and volunteering.

The work requirements were established as part of last summer's massive H.R. 1 legislation, or the One Big Beautiful Bill Act. Under the mandate, certain Medicaid applicants and beneficiaries ages 19 to 64 must meet a statutory work requirement, unless excluded. There are exemptions in place for the medically frail, disabled and others who are unable to meet the mandates.

CMS’ June 2026 interim final rule outlines qualifying activities, including 80 hours per month of work, community service or participation in a work program; enrollment in an educational program at least half-time; a combination of qualifying activities totaling at least 80 hours per month; or meeting the specified monthly income threshold.

State Medicaid administrators, health insurers, physicians and hospitals have pushed back on the Medicaid work requirements, citing concerns about financial and operational challenges, a spike in uncompensated care and the rule's strict "medical frailty" exemptions.

Industry groups argue the requirements impose onerous documentation burdens. The Alliance of Community Health Plans said that the final rule adds documentation requirements and tightens criteria for exemptions to the requirements, which will make it harder for individuals who need coverage to secure exemptions they may qualify for.

The organization also noted that most individuals enrolled in Medicaid are already working or in school.

Jennifer Tolbert, deputy director of the program on Medicaid and the uninsured at KFF, said in a quick take post back in June that "the rule adopts a restrictive definition of medical frailty that differs from states’ early expectations."

"Given the complexity of the provisions in the rule, states will likely face significant challenges in operationalizing the requirements in the next six months," Tolbert wrote. "With the short implementation timeline, states were already making implementation decisions that may not align with the rule."

Studies estimate that between 5 million and 10 million people could lose Medicaid coverage in 2028 following the rollout of new work requirements.

The Trump administration's tight definitions of medical frailty and documentation burdens have triggered multiple lawsuits from states, medical organizations and advocacy groups. A coalition of 26 states sued the Trump administration over its rollout of Medicaid work requirements. arguing that key provisions could cause eligible people to lose coverage and disrupt states’ implementation efforts. The states argue CMS’ new definition of who qualifies as “medically frail” is too restrictive and could make it harder for people with serious health conditions to receive exemptions.

In October, a Massachusetts judge sided with the states and rejected the Trump administration's definition of "medical frailty" for critical exemptions.

A group of physician groups, individual Medicaid enrollees and the city of Columbus also sued the federal government to challenge the work requirements.

State Medicaid agencies have warned that the accelerated IT and administrative build required by January 1, 2027, is putting severe pressure on local welfare offices and safety-net hospital operating margins, as Senior Writer Anastassia Gliadkovskaya reported.

Letter from the Editor-in-Chief: What a Missed Deadline Taught Me About Switching Off

📅 Nov 13, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

I missed a deadline last week. The piece you’re reading was supposed to be filed before I headed out on a long weekend to Italy for my wife’s birthday.

The main problem was I simply ran out of time to write the piece. An urgent stakeholder ask out of the blue, a couple of unanticipated work fire drills, and a long-standing evening social commitment earlier in the week made for a perfect storm, and something had to give.

The other problem and consequence was that I carried a chunk of guilt into the break.

It caught up with me at 6 am on the first morning, when I found myself drafting this column in my head. I should have been dozing peacefully. I continued to ruminate on my lengthy to-do list at work deep into day one as we headed from Venice into the Dolomites. My body was in Italy, but my brain was clearly still at my desk.

Switching off, particularly from work, is hard for most of us, and especially in an age of ubiquitous screens and wall-to-wall information competing for our attention. For me, that always-on reality plays a major role in the difficulties I have disconnecting from the daily grind.

I’m fortunate to love my job, and if I’m candid, I probably take a little too much of my identity from it. I hold on tight, and the phone in my pocket is an ever-present enabler.

A meme that has stuck with me over the years goes something along the lines of being an adult is staring at medium screens all day only to come home and stare at a little screen while a big screen is on in the background. The irony is not lost on me that I came across this rather withering take on modern life while scrolling on said little screen.

Regardless of the role the screen plays, psychologists who study employee well-being describe what I was missing for the early part of the weekend as “psychological detachment.” A state where you are genuinely able to not think about work while away from it. The science suggests it’s an important thing to work on.

A 2025 meta-analysis of 32 studies found that vacationing has a strong effect on well-being, and that the effect lasts longer than earlier research suggested. A second, smaller analysis of eight studies points to detaching from work, along with physical activity, as among the most helpful things to do while you’re off. In another study that tracked teachers during a two-week break, exhaustion, anxiety, and low mood all declined, while those who kept ruminating about work recovered less.

Whatever the science says, I know that for me the act of stepping away from work and being disciplined about not checking back in while I’m away is critical for my mental health and for staving off burnout. Getting out in nature and hiking or cycling or swimming keeps me present. It resets and recharges my batteries, and it tops up my creativity in a way that I find benefits what I am then able to bring back to the office.

Sure enough, as we drove deeper into the mountains on a crisp, blue-sky day, I could feel the golden autumnal hues and majestic rock formations drawing me in, closer to an elemental feeling of calm and happiness. That sense of equilibrium stayed with me all weekend, and while I’d be lying if I said my mind didn’t occasionally drift to my desk, it was fleeting, with the knowledge that all the things would be waiting for me when I got back. Just like this piece was.

With Thanksgiving on the horizon, and the holidays coming thick and fast thereafter, I’ll leave you with this bit of hard-earned advice: if your job is deeply entwined with your life and part of who you are, as mine is, time away can sometimes feel like a small act of disloyalty. It isn’t. In fact, it’s probably the most important thing you can do to remain faithful to your work ethic, and to yourself.

And if you’ll be working over the holidays, or you’re not working right now, or you don’t have the benefit of someone on hand to carry some of the load for you (shout out to all the Grannies out there), or your vacation looks very different from mine, that’s all okay. Find the pockets where and when you can: an evening, a walk, an hour in front of a screen, or better yet a book, and let work wait. Above all else, be kind to yourself. That’s how you really give yourself a break.

Robin Hough is the Editor-in-Chief of Healthline, and in addition to this regular Letter from the Editor column, he can be found every other Wednesday in our Healthline Wellness Wire newsletter.

How Kelly Rowland Keeps Her Eczema Flare-Ups Under Control During Winter

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Wintertime can bring everyone down with its colder weather and less sunlight. But for those with eczema, this time of year can wreak havoc on their skin, too.

That’s the case for Grammy-winning singer, actress, and Destiny’s Child alum Kelly Rowland, who has been living with eczema since her 20s. During the winter months, she often dreads the cold.

“[You] don’t know what the environment is going to give you—because mine is also environmental—or what the weather is going to give you,” Rowland told Healthline.

Cold weather and indoor heating can reduce humidity, drying out the skin and triggering or worsening eczema flare-ups.

“[What we know] about the skin barrier is that it really is like a gate or wall against the outside world,” Camille Howard-Verovic, MD, a dermatologist at Active Dermatology, told Healthline.

In healthy skin, the barrier controls what enters and leaves the skin. However, for people with eczema, Howard-Verovic said they have skin barrier dysfunction, which allows substances such as allergens and irritants to penetrate the skin.

Cold weather can increase transepidermal water loss, a process in which moisture escapes through the skin’s outer barrier and evaporates into the surrounding air.

“People with eczema have a tremendous amount of transepidermal water loss, so you’re just drier,” said Howard-Verovic. “[You’re] not holding onto the water as well, so that causes the itching and the rash that we feel when we have eczema.”

Rowland first noticed eczema flares behind her ears as well as on her elbows, arms, and the back of her knees.

“[It] looked like a rash, so that’s what I thought it was all this time, and I dealt with it for so long until after I had my son and then it started to get worse on my face,” she said.

She eventually went to a dermatologist who diagnosed her with moderate-to-severe eczema, a chronic inflammatory skin condition that can cause intense itching, painful skin cracking, thickened skin, and redness or swelling.

When Rowland learned that eczema can be genetic, it made sense to her.

“I remember seeing big rashes on [my mom’s] neck and her arms…so she had it, and I guess I was the lucky one,” said Rowland.

At first, she tried eliminating foods that she thought might be triggers. She also bathed in oatmeal.

“[I] literally have had a tub full of oatmeal, like literally went to the store to get store-bought fresh oats,” she said. “I tried everything, and it doesn’t help until you go talk to a doctor about it.”

At first, she tried prescription topical medications but found them cumbersome.

“I don’t have the time, if I’m being honest, and we live in a world that is moving so fast…I started buying like three tubes of whatever it was I was using and losing it; it was just too much for me,” Rowland said.

Rowland said she found relief with Ebglyss, a prescription injectable medication for moderate-to-severe atopic dermatitis. She has also partnered with Eli Lilly and Company, its manufacturer, to raise awareness about eczema.

Rowland said she’s sharing her experience to help others living with eczema, including people she’s met who have experienced greater challenges managing the condition.

“One lady was telling me about how she’s having a tough time [looking] in the mirror cause she doesn’t know who’s looking back at her, and she saw her skin before eczema and after,” said Rowland.

When she first had symptoms, she said she felt embarrassed, too.

“[Because] it was just in the middle of my arm space and…[if I] put out my hand to shake someone’s hand, you don’t know if somebody’s judgy,” she said.

Rowland hopes being open about her condition will encourage others to advocate for themselves and talk with their doctor about treatment options.

Because eczema can look different on deeper skin tones, Howard-Verovic noted that a dermatologist can help make the correct diagnosis.

“Some people may not even know that they have eczema,” she said. “We know that eczema is a rash; it can appear red on certain skin tones, and it can be grey or white on certain skin tones, so it can look a little different.”

In addition to medication, Rowland said managing stress helps reduce her eczema flare-ups.

“Before [when] I went on tour, because I was under so much stress, it flared up like crazy, like you wouldn’t believe,” she said. “We were in the back trying to figure out how to cover certain things up, and we figured some of it out, and some of it was really tricky, but what helped was when I actually just calmed down…not that it helped overnight, but it did just kind of bring the flare down.”

She said red light therapy, feeling grounded outside without her shoes on, getting vitamin D from natural sunlight, and cooking are ways she reduces ongoing stress.

“Cooking calms me when my kids aren’t here, and I have a really good [Zen] playlist on,” said Rowland. “[If] you ask me a question [in those moments], I might give you my heart; I’m just so happy.”

She also tries to cut back on sugar, which she believes triggers flares.

Another way to reduce flares, according to Howard-Verovic, is to avoid hot showers, especially in the winter.

“[We] know eczematous skin likes lukewarm water,” she said.

Because Howard-Verovic and her daughter both live with eczema, she set a rule about when it’s time to get out of the shower.

“[If] you see condensation on the bathroom mirror, you’ve spent too much time in the shower,” said Howard-Verovic. “[That] really hot, hot water feels really good in that moment…but when you get out all that water evaporates, and there’s no moisture in the atmosphere, and it dries your skin out, and now your skin is unhappy again.”

If you prefer to take baths, she said use gentle cleansers.

“[There] are bleach baths for really, really bad flare-ups,” she noted.

Diluted bleach baths are sometimes recommended for people with eczema, but they should only be used according to a healthcare professional’s instructions.

As soon as you get out of the shower or bath, Howard-Verovic said to apply a quality moisturizer to your damp skin. According to the National Eczema Association (NEA), lotions containing the following ingredients can help ease dry skin associated with eczema.

To help identify appropriate products, look for ones that have the NEA’s Seal of Acceptance.

Could Your GLP-1s Make You Blind? Here’s What to Make of the FDA’s Latest Notice.

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

For years, there have been rumblings and even research about a rare but serious condition that’s been linked to taking a GLP-1 medication. Now, the Food and Drug Administration is doing something about it.

The FDA just released a drug safety communication to warn patients and healthcare providers about the potential increased risk of developing non-arteritic anterior ischemic optic neuropathy (NAION) when you take a GLP-1 medication. NAION is a rare condition that can lead to vision loss in one eye, and it can be permanent.

Again, this isn't the first time concerns have been raised about the risk of NAION. But it is the first time the FDA has issued a warning about it.

Given that up to 11 percent of Americans currently take a GLP-1 for weight loss, this news stands to impact a lot of people. How concerned should you be if you're one of them? Eye experts and a doctor who prescribes GLP-1s break it down.

Meet the experts: Joseph Rizzo, MD, director of the Neuro-Ophthalmology Service at Mass Eye and Ear, a member of the Mass General Brigham healthcare system; Bavand Youssefzadeh, DO, an ophthalmologist with Cedars-Sinai in Los Angeles and a cofounder of the Global LASIK and Cataract Institute; and Mir Ali, MD, the medical director of MemorialCare Surgical Weight Loss Center at Orange Coast Medical Center in Fountain Valley, California.

The FDA's notice is designed to flag the potential risk of NAION to people who use GLP-1s. The FDA also notes that the agency will require all manufacturers of GLP-1 medications to add information about the risk of NAION to the Warnings and Precautions section of their labels.

"Patients and caregivers should be aware of a potential increased risk of NAION, a rare condition that can result in acute, painless, decreased vision in one eye, including permanent vision loss, with GLP-1 RA use," the notice reads.

The agency cited a 2024 study published in JAMA Ophthalmology as well as its own meta-analyses and retrospective cohort study in the notice. They also noted that “NAION cases have been reported for all currently marketed GLP-1 RA products.”

However, the complication remains rare: incidents from the FDA’s meta-analysis were just 6.4 events per 100,000 per year for patients using the medications.

The warning is just that—a warning to patients and physicians that this could happen, explains Joseph Rizzo, MD, director of the Neuro-Ophthalmology Service at Mass Eye and Ear, a member of the Mass General Brigham healthcare system. "Most outcome-related issues in medicine should be thought of as being probabilistic—there is a certain chance that events can occur," he says. "This warning indicates that the FDA has reason to believe, at least at this time, that the probability of NAION occurring is higher in patients who take GLP-1 RA drugs."

But Dr. Rizzo stresses that there's no evidence that these drugs cause NAION—just that there's a link.

So…why issue a warning if it's not proven that GLP-1s cause NAION and it's so rare? "The severity of a complication matters alongside its frequency," says Bavand Youssefzadeh, DO, an ophthalmologist with Cedars-Sinai in Los Angeles and a cofounder of the Global LASIK and Cataract Institute. "Even a rare risk deserves attention when it can permanently affect vision." The warning is likely designed to give healthcare providers a heads-up that it could be NAION if a patient has symptoms, he explains.

"It's their duty to report any adverse effects, just so people are aware of them," says Mir Ali, MD, the medical director of MemorialCare Surgical Weight Loss Center at Orange Coast Medical Center in Fountain Valley, California.

While NAION is scary, doctors say it's important to put the data in perspective. "It's extremely rare, but it can happen," Dr. Ali says. Given how much GLP-1s can help with blood sugar management, weight loss, and downstream health effects, Dr. Ali says that the risk of NAION is "not a reason not to seek these medications."

Dr. Rizzo agrees. "I essentially never discourage the general use of GLP-1 RA drugs," he says. "Ultimately, the risk-to-benefit assessment must be decided by the patient, but physicians should provide sufficient information to the patients to better inform their decisions."

People should stay aware of the risk without panicking, Dr. Youssefzadeh says. "It's also worth remembering that the people most likely to be prescribed these drugs—people with diabetes, high blood pressure, sleep apnea—already carry a higher baseline risk for NAION, so the risk isn't starting from zero," he adds.

American Cancer Society revises guidelines, urges routine PSA screening for prostate cancer

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

Annalisa (Nalis) Merelli is focused on boys’ and men’s health: She looks into reproductive health, trending treatments, screening approaches, mental health, and everything in between.

In a major change to screening guidelines, the American Cancer Society recommended on Thursday that American men between ages 50 and 69 at an average risk of prostate cancer should test their prostate-specific antigen (PSA) blood levels every two years.

The prior guidelines, released in 2010, left testing for average-risk men to their discretion, after assessing cancer risk with a physician.

Similarly, PSA testing recommendations were strengthened for high risk groups — Black men and men with a strong family history of prostate cancer. They are now encouraged to get PSA-based screening yearly starting at 45. Black men in particular disproportionately die from prostate cancer, and the guideline authors said they hope that increased surveillance will reduce the number of Black patients presenting with cancers at advanced stages.

Then, starting at 70, the decision on whether to continue screening becomes individualized as the balance of benefit and harm is not as clear after that age. Screening should be interrupted after 80, or in men who are not expected to live another 10 years.

The changes reflect recent findings that screening through PSA levels reduced disease specific mortality for certain men, bringing the indicator back into specialists’ good graces. Once a preferred biomarker to identify risk of prostate cancer, routine PSA screening had fallen out of favor — and was dropped from the guidelines in 2010 — for its lack of specificity, which resulted in many men undergoing follow-up procedures when they had no cancer, or very slow-growing tumors.

The U.S. Preventive Service Task Force, a federally appointed panel of outside experts, has not changed its guidelines since the new evidence became available in 2025, and continues to recommend against routine, population-wide PSA screening, suggesting instead individualized decision-making. But the panel, whose recommendations determine the preventive services that insurers must cover, has announced an upcoming update.

The cutoff for PSA levels to be considered high is established as 3.0 nanograms/milliliter or higher for men aged 45 to 69 years old. This is an important clarification, said James Eastham, chief of urology at Memorial Sloan Kettering Cancer Center. “Most PSA blood tests in the United States, when they’re reported, say under 4.0 [ng/ml] is normal, but it really should be under 3,” he said. “I think [the update] will hopefully help primary care physicians refer folks a little bit earlier.”

To limit the risk of overdiagnosis and overtreatment, the guidelines also now recommend two elevated PSA readings, followed by an MRI scan and/or testing for other biomarkers of prostate cancer, with the aim of reducing the number of patients undergoing biopsies. “We just wanted to give a little bit of flexibility for folks who cannot easily obtain an MRI,” said William Dahut, chief scientific officer at the ACS and a contributing author of the guidelines, about including both biomarkers and MRIs as a possible step following elevated PSA levels. However, he noted that an MRI would still be recommended prior to biopsy, should a biomarker test come back abnormal.

Though there are a variety of biomarkers, the guidelines don’t recommend one over the other. Dahut said ACS researchers  intend to look more closely at biomarkers to establish which may be more effective in predicting a more aggressive cancer.

“I think the new guidelines are certainly a balanced approach for early detection of prostate cancer,” said Eastham. “There have been many men undergoing unnecessary biopsies, and many low risk cancers that were subsequently overtreated,” he said. By recommending some steps between an elevated PSA and a biopsy, he said, the new recommendations reflect the evidence that while being a good biomarker, PSA alone would identify too many men as biopsy candidates.

Finally, the guidelines emphasize the importance of active surveillance in men with slow growing cancer: “If you screen more men, you’re going to find more men with early-stage disease, and we need to be strong in our position that that does not mean that you need to rush for therapies or that you need to have therapies at all.”

For Eddie Baynes, a 56-year-old prostate cancer survivor and advocate, the new guidelines “hit the nail on the head” when it comes to recommending routine PSA screening. As a Black man, he knew he was at higher risk of developing prostate cancer, yet it wasn’t until the fraternity he belonged to organized a screening event that he, at 52, started getting regular PSA tests with his bloodwork. Though routine screening wasn’t part of the ACS recommendation at the time, it led to a cancer diagnosis and a prostatectomy.

That’s why he’s been advocating for men to get PSA levels tested, and relatively frequently. With the new guidelines, men like him will start routine screening earlier than he did. “Part of my advocacy is getting tested more frequently. So now, just to have that announcement coming from the American Cancer Society, it just makes what I’ve been urging individuals to do for a while stronger,” he said.

STAT’s coverage of health challenges facing men and boys is supported by Rise Together, a donor advised fund sponsored and administered by National Philanthropic Trust and established by Richard Reeves, founding president of the American Institute for Boys and Men; and by the Boston Foundation. Our financial supporters are not involved in any decisions about our journalism.

Annalisa (Nalis) Merelli is focused on boys’ and men’s health: She looks into reproductive health, trending treatments, screening approaches, mental health, and everything in between.

Three Cheers for Gridlock

Democrats are about to get a stern lesson in the separation of powers.

Senator Bernie Sanders (I., Vt.), President Donald Trump, and Senator Elizabeth Warren (D., Mass.)

The Fight Over Obamacare’s Phantom Enrollees

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

The Trump administration is right to take on the problem of improper enrollment.

Administrator for the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services Dr. Mehmet Oz speaks during a press conference on anti-fraud initiatives at the White House campus in Washington, D.C., September 22, 2026.

More than a dozen Democratic senators are criticizing the Trump administration’s efforts to address the problem of phantom enrollments in Obamacare. In a letter to insurers, the senators accuse the companies of “colluding” with the administration to terminate coverage for more than 760,000 enrollees. Their criticism gets insurers’ financial incentives backward and overlooks the extensive safeguards the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services (CMS) employed before terminating these highly suspicious enrollments.

Insurers have little incentive to drop phantom enrollees. For years, insurers have been the biggest beneficiaries of the expanded Obamacare subsidies — reaping tens of billions of dollars in higher subsidies. By making millions of plans free to consumers, Democrats created powerful incentives for improper and phantom enrollment. Given these incentives, the administration was right to scrutinize suspicious enrollments.

Brian Blase is the president of the Paragon Health Institute. He was special assistant to the president for economic policy at the White House’s National Economic Council, 2017–19.

Faulty Wedding Cake pavilion to be dismantled at Waddesdon Manor

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Photography by Chris Lacey and Merriman Photography

A 12-metre-high pavilion shaped like a wedding cake is set to be dismantled at Waddesdon Manor in Buckinghamshire, UK, after it was closed over health and safety concerns.

A spokesperson for the Grade I-listed country house told the BBC that the pavilion, which was installed in 2023, was not “open for quite as long as hoped” due to faults in its ceramic tiles.

The sculptural pavilion, simply named Wedding Cake, was designed by Portuguese artist Joana Vasconcelos as a permanent garden pavilion for the grounds of Waddesdon Manor.

Its three tiers are wrapped in glazed, pastel-toned ceramic tiles made by the historic Portuguese tile manufacturer Viúva Lamego and intended to look like cake icing. They are now faulty, forcing the structure to close because of concerns about their safety.

According to the BBC, a report by a Buckinghamshire Council officer found that no viable solution to repair the faulty tiles had been identified.

As such, the council has approved a change to the listed building consent, allowing the sculpture to be removed. As the manor and its grounds are protected sites, permission must be obtained to carry out such work.

The structure must now be removed no later than January 2036. However, the Rothschild Foundation, which manages the estate, wants the sculpture removed as soon as possible, according to the BBC.

The revised consent also requires the surrounding landscape to be returned to grass within six months of its removal.

When the pavilion was completed, its designer Vasconcelos told Dezeen it was her “most ambitious project to date”.

Her use of tiles reflected her ambition to use ceramics and textiles to challenge traditional hierarchies of what she called “noble materials”, such as marble, in grand architectural settings.

The manufacturer Viúva Lamego’s standard 14-by-14 centimetre tiles determined the size of the overall structure. It has an 11-metre diameter, which was the smallest that could be achieved using the tiles.

The pavilion is the fourth project by Vasconcelos created for the estate, which was built in the 1870s by Baron Ferdinand de Rothschild to entertain guests and showcase his ceramic collections.

A spokesperson for the manor said it plans to continue to commission artwork for its grounds.

“We are hoping to continue to showcase art in the landscape, including plans for a new contemporary sculpture trail in the grounds of Waddesdon Manor, which will open in July 2027,” they said.

Other recent projects by Joana Vasconcelos featured on Dezeen include a 24-metre-long textile tentacle installation for Dior’s Autumn Winter 2023 catwalk show.

Elsewhere, we recently reported on the dismantling of Rike Concert Hall, designed by Italian practice Studio Fuksas. It is being demolished less than 15 years after it was completed, and before ever opening to the public.

Photography by Chris Lacey and Merriman Photography

Popular Weight-Loss Drugs May Have a Surprising Effect on Your Brain

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Popular weight-loss drugs may offer benefits that extend beyond metabolism, potentially helping preserve memory and other cognitive abilities.

Medications used to treat obesity and type 2 diabetes may also influence memory and other cognitive abilities. But whether drugs such as semaglutide and tirzepatide can actually protect the brain remains uncertain, particularly after major clinical trials failed to demonstrate a benefit in patients with early Alzheimer’s disease.

In Novo Nordisk’s EVOKE and EVOKE+ trials, semaglutide did not slow Alzheimer’s progression, even though it produced changes in several biological markers associated with the disease. Researchers from the University of Gothenburg and the University of Copenhagen have now examined the broader evidence in a review published in Science, exploring whether these medications could still help preserve cognitive function in other groups of patients.

GLP-1 medications influence biological processes involved in both metabolism and brain function. Animal experiments suggest they can affect learning and memory, but studies involving humans have produced inconsistent results. Analyses of patient health records have linked GLP-1 treatment to a reduced risk of dementia among people with type 2 diabetes, although these observational findings cannot establish that the drugs directly provide protection.

The potential benefits may differ depending on the cause of cognitive difficulties. People with obesity or type 2 diabetes sometimes experience problems with attention, planning, and mental processing speed. These difficulties are frequently associated with metabolic disturbances and poor vascular health, raising the possibility that treatments targeting these underlying problems could help maintain cognitive abilities.

Stina Börchers, the review’s first author and a researcher affiliated with both universities, believes that identifying the right patients and treatment timing will be essential.

“We need to find out whether these treatments can help people with obesity or diabetes preserve their cognitive abilities, particularly if treatment begins before cognitive problems become established. It is also important to identify which patients might benefit,” says Stina Börchers.

A central unanswered question is whether any cognitive benefits arise from direct effects on the brain, improvements in metabolic health, or both. Better regulation of blood sugar and other metabolic processes could indirectly support brain function, while GLP-1 signaling may also influence neural processes more directly. Separating these mechanisms could help researchers determine which effects are most relevant to preserving cognition.

Christoffer Clemmensen, the review’s senior author and a research group leader at the University of Copenhagen, sees an opportunity to use increasingly advanced obesity medications to investigate these connections.

“Obesity medicines are becoming increasingly sophisticated, offering new opportunities to understand how metabolic regulation influences brain function. By disentangling the direct and indirect effects of these drugs on the brain, we may ultimately be able to develop treatments specifically aimed at preserving cognitive function,” says Christoffer Clemmensen.

The researchers are calling for clinical trials that measure memory and other cognitive abilities from the beginning of treatment. Such studies could help establish whether these medications preserve cognitive function, which patients are most likely to benefit, and whether starting treatment before cognitive difficulties appear makes a difference.

They also caution that potential benefits must be weighed against risks in older or frail patients. Rapid weight loss and declining muscle mass can reduce physical functioning, potentially limiting the overall advantages of treatment. Future studies will need to account for these effects when assessing whether obesity medications can support long-term brain health.

Reference: “Obesity pharmacotherapies and cognition” by Stina Börchers, Giulia Mazzini, Henrik Zetterberg and Christoffer Clemmensen, 5 November 2026, Science. DOI: 10.1126/science.aek9987

High Fat Diet Linked to Higher Colon Cancer Risk, Early Research Shows

📅 Nov 12, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

In a new study, researchers report that a high fat diet may potentially increase the risk of colon cancer.

The researchers from Arizona State University said a diet high in certain fats can cause lasting changes in intestinal stem cells that lead to the development of tumors and the possibility of colon cancer.

The study was conducted in mice, but the researchers noted changes in the DNA of intestinal stem cells that may explain their growth and survival.

The researchers published their findings on November 12 in the journal Stem Cell Reports.

The researchers said the study is important because 1 in 25 people develop colon cancer in their lifetime. In the United States, that translates into about 108,000 new cases of colon cancer every year. About 55,000 males and 55,000 females are expected to die from the disease in 2026.

Experts not involved in the study say the research provides valuable information, but they have reservations about the results, as the findings do not yet apply to humans.

“The findings provide a potential mechanism linking diet to intestinal cancer development, but we need to be cautious about translating these results to humans,” said Dalia Perelman, MS, CDE, a research dietitian at Stanford University in California.

“We already have evidence from human studies that obesity and certain dietary patterns, particularly those high in processed meats and low in fiber-rich foods, are associated with increased colorectal cancer risk,” Perelman told Healthline. “This study adds a potentially important mechanism to investigate, but it does not establish that dietary fat itself increases colorectal cancer risk in humans.”

Ketan Thanki, MD, a colorectal surgeon specializing in benign and malignant diseases of the colon, rectum, and anus at the MemorialCare Todd Cancer Institute at Long Beach Medical Center in California, shared similar views.

“This study provides a better understanding at the cellular level of how a high fat diet may contribute to colorectal cancer development,” Thanki told Healthline.

“Animal studies are an important starting point for understanding biological processes, but we must be cautious about directly applying their findings to humans,” he added. “Mice and humans share many cellular and biological mechanisms, which makes these studies valuable in identifying potential pathways involved in disease development. However, human diets, genetics, metabolism, and environmental exposures are more complex.”

To reach their findings, the Arizona State researchers fed mice what the scientists described as a Western-style high fat diet for 6 months.

They then compared those results with those from mice given a low fat diet.

The researchers said the mice on the high fat diet gained more weight than the mice on the low-fat diet. They also developed more intestinal stem cells, and those cells grew at a more rapid rate.

They added that mice genetically predisposed to intestinal cancer who were fed a high-fat diet developed more and larger tumors.

The researchers noted changes in the DNA of intestinal stem cells that may help explain their increased growth and survival.

They also pointed out that stem cell behavior returned to normal after high fat diet mice were switched to a low fat diet, although some DNA changes persisted. After four weeks, approximately 18% of these changes remained.

When mice were returned to a high fat diet, the researchers said their intestinal stem cells responded more quickly and strongly than those of mice exposed to the diet for the first time.

They concluded that the study suggests that maintaining a healthy, low fat diet may help regulate intestinal stem cell growth and reduce colon cancer risk.

Kristin Kirkpatrick, RD, president of KAK Consulting and a dietitian at the Cleveland Clinic Department of Wellness & Preventive Medicine in Ohio, wasn’t involved in the study but highlighted a particular aspect of the findings.

“What I found particularly interesting was that even after the mice returned to a low fat diet, some of the molecular changes persisted,” she told Healthline. “This raises questions about how our dietary patterns over time may influence long-term health.”

Experts said these new findings are interesting but noted numerous caveats.

“One important takeaway is that dietary habits over time matter,” said Thanki. “Even if someone has consumed a high fat diet for years, making changes now may still provide meaningful health benefits.”

“I also think these findings are particularly important for children and adolescents, as establishing healthier dietary habits early in life may provide long-term benefits,” he added.

However, Thanki noted that not all fats are the same. “The health risks depend partly on the type of fat consumed, how much is consumed, and what foods make up the overall diet,” he said.

“Diets consistently high in saturated fats, particularly those containing large amounts of processed meats, fried foods, and highly processed products, can contribute to obesity, elevated cholesterol, cardiovascular disease, and metabolic dysfunction. These factors can also contribute to chronic inflammation and may increase the risk of certain cancers, including colorectal cancer,” Thanki continued.

Perelman said the diet used in the study was extremely high in fat, providing 60% of calories from fat, primarily from lard.

“We know that the health effects of dietary fats differ depending on their sources and fatty acid composition, and we cannot assume that a diet rich in unsaturated fats from foods such as olive oil, nuts, and seeds would produce the same effects,” she noted.

Perelman added that the researchers primarily studied stem cells from the small intestine rather than the colon.

“Although these tissues share some biological characteristics, they have important differences, and we cannot assume that the same changes would occur in the colon,” she explained.

Finally, Perelman noted that “the mice consuming the high fat diet also gained weight, making it difficult to separate the effects of dietary fat itself from those associated with increased body fat and other metabolic changes.”

Not all high fat foods are unhealthy. Some high fat foods that offer healthy benefits include:

“The goal shouldn’t necessarily be to eliminate fat because our bodies need healthy fats for normal functioning,” said Thanki. “Instead, people should focus on reducing saturated and trans fats while incorporating healthier unsaturated fats in appropriate amounts.”

Thanki had several dietary suggestions. Among them:

“Consistency matters more than perfection. Even modest dietary changes, maintained over time, can have meaningful health benefits,” Thanki said.

Perelman agreed. “The overall quality of the diet matters. Diets that are very high in fat may displace other foods and nutrients that have important health benefits,” she said.

“For colorectal cancer specifically, higher intakes of dietary fiber and whole grains have consistently been associated with lower risk,” Perelman noted.

“Therefore, rather than focusing exclusively on reducing fat, it is important to consider the overall dietary pattern and whether it provides adequate amounts of fiber-rich plant foods and other beneficial nutrients.”

Kirkpatrick agreed that the type of fat is what is important. “A Mediterranean-style diet that includes olive oil, nuts, seeds, and fatty fish can be relatively high in fat but is consistently associated with positive health outcomes,” she said.

“On the other hand, a diet high in saturated fat, processed meats, and ultra-processed foods may increase the risk of certain chronic diseases, particularly when it’s also low in fiber and other protective nutrients.”

Kirkpatrick also noted that many lifestyle habits contribute to a person’s overall health.

“Our health is influenced by so many factors beyond diet, including how we manage stress, our sleep habits, physical activity, genetics, and overall lifestyle,” she said.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/hospitals/bjc-health-st-lukes-reveal-plans-merge" hreflang="en">BJC Health, St. Luke's reveal plans to merge</a>

📅 2026-11-11  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
BJC Health, St. Luke's reveal plans to merge" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Missouri-based BJC Health and St. Luke's Hospital are planning a merger.

The providers announced on Wednesday that they have signed a letter of intent to come together, bringing the community hospital into the fold with the Show Me State's largest health system. The pair expects the deal to improve access and improve local care in the St. Louis region.

St. Luke's said in the announcement that the letter "follows a deliberate and thorough evaluation process," conducted by its board of directors and leaders "to identify the best path forward for St. Luke’s and the communities it serves."

The providers said they intend to spend the next several months building a definitive agreement to merge and complete the needed regulatory approval process. In the interim, St. Luke's patients can continue to see their regular physicians.

BJC said in the announcement that it is committed to supporting local, community care while also allowing St. Luke's to tap into the resources available across its ecosystem. It intends to provide key support for the hospital's clinical technology and equipment as well as backing infrastructure and facility upgrades and provider recruitment and retention efforts.

St. Luke’s is a well-respected hospital, known for clinical quality, patient experience and the personal attention given to everyone in our care,” said Andy Bagnall, CEO of St. Luke’s, in the announcement. “At the same time, the demands of health care delivery and our communities’ needs are changing rapidly."

We believe that together with BJC, we can strengthen patient care in St. Louis in ways we could not easily do on our own," Bagnall said.

Nick Barto, CEO of BJC Health, called St. Luke's "a trusted leader in our region with a reputation for excellence."

"Together, we will build on that foundation through our aligned clinical strengths and complementary services," Barto said in the announcement. "Our shared goal is to expand access to differentiated, high-quality care while ensuring St. Luke’s can continue serving the growing communities of West St. Louis for generations to come.”

Can Creativity Make You Happier?

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 11, 2026 | Reviewed by Gary Drevitch

The game show Family Feud pits two families against each other in a race to find the most popular answers to silly questions like “Name something you might find in a bathtub.” If you guessed “water” and 50 of the 100 people who had been surveyed had also chosen that answer, you would get those points. If you answered “arachnid,” you might get zero points. Even though many children learned the "Itsy-Bitsy Spider" song growing up, "arachnid" would probably not be one of the most-popular answers.

Creative, unconventional, divergent thinkers might be at a disadvantage on Family Feud. Yet research demonstrates that generating creative, original thought and activity can make you happier (Tan, et al. 2021).

Moderate stress, like what you might feel when taking a test, can improve our convergent thinking—the type of thinking that's required when we want to find the right answer to a question or a problem. However, stress can stifle divergent, out-of-the-box, creative problem-solving. Divergent thinking involves generating multiple novel ideas from multiple perspectives and/or combinations of perspectives (Guo et al., 2024). When we feel looser, freer, and more relaxed, we can access our creative mind more readily.

Creativity can lower stress by offering outlets for strong emotions. Poets, authors, songwriters, dancers, and other artists find that strong emotions can fuel creativity as they strive to transform pain into beauty. Psychotherapists often recommend creative writing, journaling, and other artistic outlets as a therapeutic intervention. Creativity can increase well-being, providing opportunities to feel more fully alive and joyful (Tan, et al. 2021).

Every month, a group of friends gathers at our home for a musical jam session. We improvise songs, switch instruments, and often end by drumming on percussion instruments. This shared creativity strengthens our friendship and our sense of playfulness. Engaging in creative arts with others reduces loneliness and supports social connection, an essential requirement for happiness (Perkins, et al. 2021).

Many young people have formed deep friendships from Dungeons and Dragons, Magic: The Gathering, and similar role-playing games that require creative imagination, divergent thinking, and sometimes even elaborate costume creations.

Studies show that group-based arts and creativity programs can improve the health, well-being, and quality of life of older people. These activities strengthen social ties, encourage an active imagination, and promote physical activity, all essential for a healthy life (Sowerby, et al. 2026).

When I suggest to clients that they might improve their happiness by finding a creative outlet, I often hear, “But I’m not a creative person.” I usually ask them a few questions like, “Have you ever substituted a different ingredient in a recipe when you didn’t have the right ingredients? Or have you ever had to try different ways to teach your child how to tie shoelaces?” Typically, people can think of several times in the recent past when they have been creative.

Author, psychotherapist, and writing coach Marni Freedman writes in her delightful new book, Artistic Badassery, “The latest research on creativity from both a psychological and a neuroscientific perspective shows that creativity is not an inborn gift or a fixed trait, but rather a set of skills that can be developed through practice, effort, and the right conditions.”

Like any mental muscle, creativity needs exercise to develop. If you’re just learning to play guitar, you may not yet have enough musical vocabulary to improvise. Yet you can write a song after learning only three chords. All you need is an idea and a melody. It can be fun to create something original, even if it wouldn’t pass critical muster. Try making something just for the fun of it, without criticizing the outcome. Let yourself play in the sandbox of creativity. It might make you happier.

I think there should be a Family Feud game for divergent thinkers. You would try to pick the most novel answers to the questions asked, and the person who picks logical items that no one else chooses wins. Here’s a fun question to start: “What items might be found in a national ballroom?”

Freedman, M. 2026. Artistic Badassery: How to Unblock and Become a Creative Force of Nature. Hay House LLC.

Perkins R, Mason-Bertrand A, Tymoszuk U, Spiro N, Gee K, Williamon A. Arts engagement supports social connectedness in adulthood: findings from the HEartS Survey. BMC Public Health. 2021 Jun 24;21(1):1208. doi: 10.1186/s12889-021-11233-6. PMID: 34162360; PMCID: PMC8221987.

Sowerby EJ, Doherty A, Harrison J. Arts and creativity and its impact on older people's health and wellbeing: commentary on a systematic review. Pract Nurs. 2025 Mar 2;36(3):98-103. doi: 10.12968/pnur.2024.0032. PMID: 41503028; PMCID: PMC7618590.

Tan CY, Chuah CQ, Lee ST, Tan CS. Being Creative Makes You Happier: The Positive Effect of Creativity on Subjective Well-Being. Int J Environ Res Public Health. 2021 Jul 6;18(14):7244. doi: 10.3390/ijerph18147244. PMID: 34299693; PMCID: PMC8305859.

Xiaoyu Guo, Yifan Wang, Yuecui Kan, Jiaqi Zhang, Linden J. Ball, Haijun Duan, How does stress shape creativity? The mediating effect of stress hormones and cognitive flexibility, Thinking Skills and Creativity, Volume 52,2024,101521, ISSN 1871-1871, doi.org/10.1016/j.tsc.2024.101521.
