ABA President Barbara Howard says association will continue to prioritize the defense of democracy

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

It’s been a busy few months for Barbara Howard.

As the ABA’s president, Howard has worked to protect voting rights and urged her fellow members to do the same. She vowed to fight assaults on civil rights, which include critical threats facing unaccompanied children and other immigrants. She also spent three weeks in Europe, where she talked with lawyers in London and Copenhagen about the association’s efforts to defend the rule of law.

Howard, principal of Barbara J. Howard Co. in Cincinnati, is serving a one-year term that ends in August. She has a long history of leadership in the association, which includes being a member of the House of Delegates since 1986. She is also a past chair of the House.

In a conversation with the ABA Journal on Monday, Howard talked more about her first tasks as ABA president and what she plans to focus on in the months ahead. Our interview has been edited for length and clarity.

It’s been a few months since you became the ABA’s president, and about a week since the midterm elections. You’ve said that you want ABA members to help protect the right to vote. Why is that important?

I’ve put out two statements, one in September and one in October, and in both of them, I pointed to the role that we as citizens play in our democracy. As many before us have said, with democracy, it’s not one and done. It needs to be actively pursued and defended every day. It’s not the kind of thing where you can just sit back on your laurels and say, “Well, we’ve got our democracy.” And we all realized that. I think that’s what we saw as a country.

Voting is one of the most important responsibilities of an American citizen. It’s how we give our citizens the opportunity to hold our lawmakers and our officials accountable, and it’s how we have our voices heard. So for me, the focus was very much on getting out messages and encouraging lawyers around the country to talk with their fellow citizens, to engage with them, to assure them that our voting systems are very secure and that they should exercise the right to vote.

How have you been involved in those efforts?

Well, I spent Election Day as a poll worker. This was my fifth year. I worked the same polling place as I did last year. So that means on Monday night spending an hour, two hours setting up all the equipment, getting everything ready. Then it was about a 15-hour day, from roughly 5:15 in the morning until 8:15 at night. And we had probably about a 25% increase in the number of people who voted from the year before.

You’ve also expressed a need for the ABA to continue its fight against attacks on civil rights. What has that work looked like so far?

So far this year I’ve really been focused on the elections because we’ve had to be. As we now move past the elections, we need to be sure all the members of Congress are able to take their seats in January.

Another issue right now is immigration. We have seen some very negative developments with respect to the representation of especially unaccompanied minor children, but immigrants in general. ProBAR has had its funds pulled, and the entity with whom it contracts, Acacia, has had its grant terminated. There has been some money generated to allow ProBAR to set up a hotline for unaccompanied minor children to call in to get direction in terms of how they can get legal representation, which is required by Congress. That’s called the DORY hotline. Shelters have refused to give the phone number to minors who have asked for it. That’s a matter that is going to need some immediate attention.

There is also the overall issue with the U.S. Department of Health and Human Services saying that rather than award a new grant to Acacia or someone else, it is going to internalize its representation so that the government is both representing the minor children and making the decisions on who stays and goes. That is an apparent, if not actual, conflict of interest. There’s a letter going out to Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. urging him to reassess that whole process. We will continue to monitor that very, very closely.

Turning to the ABA itself, many of its leaders have focused on how to make the association more efficient and serve its members better. What changes do you hope to see moving forward?

The Board of Governors has taken on what we’re calling “the renovation project.” It has become apparent as I’ve sat through several budget cycles and in talking to incoming section, division and forum chairs and staff directors, as well as others in the ABA, that we are too complex. We’ve gotten so complex that it’s made it really hard for new members, especially younger lawyers and potential new members, to navigate the ABA. When they get frustrated, they just give up. Because we’re seeing the nonrenewal rate of first-year lawyers is so much greater than for the rest of the association population, it’s crying out for reform.

We have five teams that are making their phone calls to entities, whether it’s a section, division, forum or a standing or special committee or commission. We are looking at all aspects of the association to see where we can make some changes that hopefully will simplify the structure, eliminate both silos and duplication of effort, and make it easier for people to find their way to their home or homes. We will be having an open forum at the midyear meeting for people to express their thoughts and ideas. We will really start working right after midyear on some proposals for revising our structure.

The first goal of the strategic plan is that the ABA is a home for all lawyers. So this is to help make that happen. The third goal is that we have a strong financial foundation to take us forward into our next 150 years as an association. So this is also to help in that effort.

I can imagine that is a big priority. Are there any others you’d like to mention?

The other one is the middle goal of the strategic plan, which is that we are the voice of the legal profession and defender of the rule of law. I have spoken a lot about the rule of law, both across the country and overseas. Lawyers overseas are very, very focused on what’s happening in the United States. We carry a great weight with our colleagues, and it’s important to them that they know what we’re doing to make sure that our democracy thrives in the face of some real attacks.

I have no preconceived idea that we are on an easy street now that the elections are passed. I think there will be continued efforts to dismantle parts of our democracy, and we will continue to defend it. That will certainly be a priority.

What have you enjoyed about the job and found most fun?

I’ve really enjoyed talking with people and helping create solutions. That is what we as lawyers do best—we solve problems. So helping advance the mission of the ABA and solve problems for either lawyers or entities within the ABA has been very rewarding. And I’ve certainly enjoyed traveling and getting to know both ABA members, and again, our colleagues around the world who are very much interested in the work of the ABA.

You’ve been an ABA member for a long time, and you know a lot of people well. But what is one thing other members might not know about you?

I am a third-generation lawyer. My maternal grandfather was also mayor of the city of Toledo, where I grew up, for 10 years. As a child, it was kind of fun walking down the street with him. People would greet him as Mr. Mayor. And then my paternal grandfather was not only successful in his practice, but he was also appointed as a probate court judge for a while.

STAT+: Cassidy sets key vote on Overton, even as he remains undecided on nominee to lead FDA

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Margaret is a Washington correspondent covering the Food and Drug Administration and other federal health agencies. Reach her on Signal at margaretmanto.61.

WASHINGTON — The Senate health committee has scheduled a pivotal vote for next week on the nomination of Heidi Overton to serve as the next commissioner of the Food and Drug Administration — despite lingering doubts from Republicans on the panel.

The vote is set for Nov. 18, according to a committee release. If it passes, Overton’s nomination would then go to the full Senate, where Republicans are racing to confirm nominees before Democrats take power in January. The committee will also vote on James O’Neill’s nomination to serve as director of the National Science Foundation and three other nominations.

Even as he scheduled the vote, committee Chair Bill Cassidy (R-La.) remained undecided on Overton, he wrote in a post on X. It would take just one Republican on the committee to sink her nomination, assuming all Democrats vote against her.

Is Pilates Effective For Weight Loss? Instructors Share How To Incorporate It Into Your Routine

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

It’s nearly impossible to scroll through social media right now without seeing some celebrity or influencer rave about the perks of Pilates. But even with all the buzz surrounding this popular practice, details are a little fuzzy on whether Pilates is an effective workout for weight loss.

First off, know that the health and fitness benefits of having a Pilates practice are so much more than that. This form of exercise is known for improving your posture, breathing, flexibility, and core strength. As Claudette Sariya, CPT, a personal trainer and fitness educator based in New York City, notes, “any movement that you can enjoy regularly and consistently can help you lose weight.”

So, doing Pilates (or any workout for that matter) to stay active paired with a nutrition plan is likely to help you with weight loss. But it probably won't help expedite your weight loss in the same way that, say, lifting heavy weights would. (We'll get into why!)

The focus of Pilates is not weight loss, experts point out. "Pilates should be considered part of your self-care routine," says Jacquelyn Brennan, CSCS, pilates instructor and co-founder of Chicago-based Pilates and Coffee. "We all need to move our spines more, move our joints through every range of motion, and everyone needs to be breathing better."

All of those healthy practices together can aid weight loss, though. Here's everything to know about doing Pilates when you're trying to lose weight.

Meet the experts: Gabbi Berkow, RD, CPT, is a nutritionist and Pilates instructor. Jacquelyn Brennan, CSCS, is a Pilates instructor and co-founder of Chicago-based Pilates and Coffee. Claudette Sariya, CPT, is a personal trainer and fitness educator based in New York City. Sonja R. Price Herbert is a certified Pilates instructor and founder of Black Girl Pilates. Nofar Hagag is a Pilates instructor and creator of the Nofar Method.

One form of Pilates isn't superior to another to drop pounds, experts agree. "For weight loss, I don’t think one type of Pilates is necessarily best,” says Berkow. Yet, there are different flavors—and finding one that you enjoy and will keep coming back to can, in turn, help you commit to a fitness routine and lose weight.

When someone mentions Pilates, they're often referring to Romana’s Pilates, which features exercises that Joseph Pilates (the founder) developed, explains Gabbi Berkow, RD, CPT, a nutritionist and Pilates instructor. Some studios are dedicated to classical or Romana’s Pilates (which is what Berkow is trained in) but there are studios that feature different Pilates-based workouts, which means they use Pilates-inspired moves but don't follow the classical version to a T. Some Pilates classes take place on a mat (a.k.a. mat Pilates), while others occur on a reformer, which is basically a sliding, spring-loaded platform.

You also might hear the term "athletic Pilates," but that is usually a description of the pace, says Sonja R. Price Herbert, a certified Pilates instructor and founder of Black Girl Pilates. These classes are designed to be fast-paced and high-intensity, and may even have some high-intensity interval training (HIIT) exercises and elements in the mix, which would make them better for weight loss due to the way HIIT training can help you burn more calories during and after the workout.

But if you really want to maximize your Pilates practice for weight loss and strength, it’s a good idea to use both the reformer and Cadillac machines, says Nofar Hagag, a Pilates instructor and creator of the Nofar Method. “By incorporating cardio-focused movements—such as squats, planks, and Pilates burpees, your heart rate increases, boosting calorie burn and metabolism,” she says. “These high-energy moves are paired with strength-training exercises, like side leg lifts and abs teasers, to build lean muscle and improve body composition.” (Reminder: Loading your muscles with resistance is key for muscle growth—and more muscle mass helps you burn more calories at rest and, in turn, lose weight.)

What you also want when it comes to Pilates and weight loss: an instructor who knows what they’re doing, is committed to safe movements, and who will challenge you, says Berkow. “You want to make sure your Pilates class is a workout. It shouldn’t be just a series of slow, easy stretches. Pilates has to contribute to your total daily calorie burn for it to make a difference in weight loss,” she says.

If you’ve ever gone to a class, you know how insane the core work is (seriously, the famed Hundreds exercise is not for the faint of heart...or abs).

While you cannot spot-reduce fat, Pilates can help you learn how to properly engage your pelvic floor and deep core muscles, says Brennan. "Pilates will teach you how to engage your muscles correctly, which will lend to increased activation during core exercises and lead to [the appearance of a] 'tighter tummy,' " she says.

However, to see abs definition, you do have to lose the fat that sits on top of your muscles, which requires proper nutrition, says Berkow. Think: a diet rich in whole, fresh foods and a limit on overly-processed foods and snacks.

There is some research to suggest that you can lose weight doing Pilates—but it's tough to give an exact number of pounds considering so many individual factors play into weight loss, such as genetics, sleep and stress, lifestyle habits, and more.

A meta-analysis of 11 randomized controlled trials published in Frontiers in Physiology in 2021 found that pilates “dramatically” reduced body weight and body fat percentage in adults with overweight or obesity. And the longer the person practiced Pilates, the bigger the weight loss. Older research has had mixed results, though, with one small study concluding Pilates was indeed helpful for weight loss, while another found it had no impact on weight loss in post-menopausal women.

You want a mat that offers a little extra cushion than the standard yoga mat to protect your vertebrae when rolling onto your back.

ProBody Pilates Ring And Mini Exercise Ball

Pilates teachers love a good prop. You may want to have a few options—like this Pilates ring (a.k.a. magic circle) and ball set—handy for online classes.

Many studios require sticky socks for both safety and sanitary reasons, so stashing a pair in your gym bag is always a good idea. These Pointe Studio socks offer a non-slip bottom in tons of cute colors and patterns.

Looking for a mat that's a bit more aesthetic? The 8mm-sized Bala Play Mat is another cushy option (and it’s antimicrobial, to boot!).

If you want to invest in your Pilates practice, this home reformer is a cult favorite that often shows up in professional studios. During testing, we found this machine to have a sleek, low profile, sturdy frame, and impressive footbar adjustability.

Again, traditional Pilates isn’t a modality designed for weight loss. Generally, traditional classes do not pack enough of a cardio or strength training component, Herbert says: “It is not a steady-state [cardio modality] like running, jumping, or walking. There is also not enough weight [or resistance] to aid in a decrease in body fat.” Instead, Herbert says that “Pilates focuses on awareness of the body, efficiency of movement, and breath.”

Pilates will, however, deliver far more benefits than just a lower number on a scale that may indirectly help with weight loss. For instance, Pilates involves breath work, encouraging deeper belly breaths that are in direct contrast to the shallow breathing many of us do on the regular. “Shallow breathing leads to increased cortisol levels, which contributes to weight gain. One could say that through pilates and learning to breathe better, you’d have lower cortisol—and thus, stress—levels, something that can help you lose weight,” Brennan says.

Feeling better, both mentally and physically, will also encourage you to take care of your body, says Berkow—and Pilates can definitely be a key part of achieving an overall weight-loss goal.

Jessica Migala is a health writer specializing in general wellness, fitness, nutrition, and skincare, with work published in Women’s Health, Glamour, Health, Men’s Health, and more. She is based in the Chicago suburbs and is a mom to two little boys and rambunctious rescue pup.

Korin Miller is a seasoned freelance writer specializing in general wellness, sexual health and relationships, and lifestyle trends, with work appearing in Men’s Health, Women’s Health, Everyday Health, Food & Wine, and more. <a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/payers/carelon-agrees-pay-10m-settle-ghost-network-coverage-denial-allegations-new-york" hreflang="en">Carelon agrees to pay $10M to settle ghost network, coverage denial allegations in New York</a>

📅 2026-11-11  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
Carelon agrees to pay $10M to settle ghost network, coverage denial allegations in New York" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Carelon will pay more than $10 million across two settlements in New York over mental health networks and coverage denials.

The company, a division of Elevance Health, will pay $2.35 million in penalties, fees and costs as well as up to $8 million in restitution for members who paid out-of-pocket for out-of-network mental healthcare, to reimburse those whose claims were improperly denied or to overhaul its networks.

New York Attorney General Letitia James said in an announcement that the settlements resolve two investigations from her office that determined the company misled members by directing them to seek care from providers that were out-of-network—through so-called "ghost networks"—and improperly denied coverage for treatment for substance abuse disorder.

James noted that Carelon administers behavioral health benefits for multiple plans, including Anthem Blue Cross, EmblemHealth, Independent Health and the Empire Plan, which covers state and local government workers.

Across these insurers, Carelon manages behavioral health benefits for more than 4 million people in the state, James said.

The alleged behavior also goes against a 2015 agreement with the AG's office, where the company agreed that it would maintain accurate provider networks and "use approved criteria" when denying substance abuse treatments.

"When someone is ready to seek help for a mental health condition or addiction, every day matters," said James. “Carelon left New Yorkers calling provider after provider who could not see them and made it harder for people to get the treatment to which they were entitled."

"These settlements will help people get the care they need and put money back in New Yorkers’ pockets," she said.

Elevance Health said in a statement to Fierce Healthcare that it is committed to ensuring patients have access to the most up-to-date provider information.

"Carelon Behavioral Health is committed to helping people find and access high-quality mental health and substance use disorder care. Accurate, up-to-date provider information is an important part of that effort," the company said. "We continue to invest in tools and processes that support accurate provider information and work closely with providers and other partners to help connect consumers with the care they need."

Carelon was hit with a lawsuit in April over the alleged ghost networks and their impact on the Empire Plan in particular. In the case, plaintiff's attorneys conducted secret shopper calls to analyze Carelon's network, calling 300 providers. Just 17% of those contacted took insurance and were open to new patients.

These "ghost networks" have also drawn concern in other markets, such as for maternal care services in Medicaid and in Medicare Advantage. A study released last month found that commercial plans also routinely have inaccurate provider directories.

1 in 3 Infants Diagnosed With Peanut Allergy May Not Have the Condition

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Peanut allergies have been on the rise in recent decades.

According to statistics provided by Food Allergy Research & Education (FARE), an estimated 6.2 million children and adults in the United States are allergic to peanuts.

However, new research published in the November 2026 issue of The Journal of Allergy and Clinical Immunology suggests that over a third of infants thought to have this diagnosis might not actually have a peanut allergy.

In fact, having a history of reacting to peanuts and positive allergy testing on their own may not be sufficient to diagnose a peanut allergy for many infants.

The study’s authors suggest that a threshold oral food challenge — in which the child is given increasing amounts of the suspected food allergen under medical supervision to look for an allergic reaction — should be routinely considered to help confirm peanut allergy.

This additional testing could safely prevent children who are not allergic to peanuts from undergoing unnecessary treatment with oral immunotherapy.

It could also spare families from burdensome food restrictions and allergy precautions.

The researchers’ goal was to evaluate the accuracy and safety of the Allergy Development to an Accelerated Path to Tolerance (ADAPT) peanut oral immunotherapy program, which was established in 2024 at 10 public children’s hospitals across Australia.

This program provides a standardized approach to evaluating and treating infants with suspected peanut allergies.

The study included infants who had experienced a mild to moderate reaction to peanut before 12 months of age and had evidence of peanut sensitization through allergy testing.

Researchers reviewed medical records to examine how accurately the program’s approach identified peanut allergies and whether it could safely guide treatment.

A key part of the evaluation was a threshold oral food challenge. The challenge involved up to seven doses, ranging from 15 to 1,300 milligrams of peanut protein.

Medical teams monitored the infants for reactions and provided treatment when necessary.

Researchers reviewed records from April 2024 through October 2025. During that period, 1,275 infants were assessed through the program, and 778 underwent an oral food challenge.

The researchers also examined allergy test results and whether infants who reacted during the challenges could begin oral immunotherapy at an appropriate starting dose.

At the time of the oral food challenge, the infants had a median age of 13 months.

Of the 778 infants who underwent an oral food challenge, 507 (65.2%) had an allergic reaction.

However, 271 (34.8%) did not react to peanut during the supervised test, despite having a history of a previous reaction and positive allergy test results.

These findings suggest that relying on medical history and allergy tests alone may lead some infants to be labeled with a peanut allergy when they can actually tolerate the food.

Among the infants who reacted during the challenges, 61 experienced anaphylaxis, a severe allergic reaction that can affect breathing and blood pressure. This represented 12% of infants with a positive challenge and 7.8% of all infants who underwent testing. Medical teams safely managed these severe allergic reactions.

Researchers also found that infants who reacted to peanut generally had higher skin prick test results and Ara h 2-specific IgE levels than those who did not react.

However, these test results did not reliably indicate how much peanut would trigger a reaction or how severe that reaction would be.

Of the 394 infants who began peanut oral immunotherapy, 384 (97.5%) successfully started treatment at a dose below the amount that had triggered a reaction during their food challenge.

The researchers say these findings support considering supervised oral food challenges to confirm suspected peanut allergies before starting treatment or recommending long-term avoidance.

Erin Banta, MD, a board certified allergist and immunologist who treats both children and adults at Latitude Food Allergy Care in Great Neck, New York, said these findings are significant because they provide evidence for early use of oral food challenges in infants with peanut reactions.

“In those with mild-moderate prior reactions to peanut, early oral food challenge may allow for either delabeling of food allergy (in 1/3 of the patients in this study) or diagnosis of food allergy,” she told Healthline.

Banta, who wasn’t involved in the study, added that early delabeling takes a significant burden off the patient and their family.

“On the other hand,” she explained, “early diagnosis allows for early initiation of treatment with oral immunotherapy at a time in the patient’s life associated with the greatest success rates.”

Banta further noted that the study offers different pathways for oral immunotherapy based on the patient’s reaction threshold. This means that some patients may be able to complete treatment more quickly due to their individual threshold dose.

Lindsey Forstie, FNP, a family nurse practitioner with clinical training and experience in allergy and asthma care at Carefree Allergy in Phoenix, AZ, added that while further evaluation may be needed to confirm a peanut allergy diagnosis, it’s important for parents not to try introducing peanuts at home.

“Instead, they should speak with their pediatrician or allergist about appropriate testing and whether a supervised food challenge is safe and necessary,” she said.

According to Forstie, who was also not part of the study, the goal is to avoid unnecessary food restrictions while also keeping children safe.

“The key message is that not every positive allergy test means a child has a food allergy,” she told Healthline. “Accurate diagnosis helps protect children while allowing those who are not allergic to enjoy a more normal, less restrictive diet.”

DW Science explainer on the default mode network: what your brain does when you aren't focusing on anything, and why it's important.

📅 Nov 11, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Doing nothing might be the ultimate brain hack. When you're bored, the brain goes into default mode network. It's not only healthy for you, but it may also shape the very essence of your identity.

You know when you lock your phone and the screen goes black? It seems like it's not doing anything. But it's not doing nothing. It's syncing your messages, updating apps, and running other background processes.

Imagine you're sitting on a bus, staring out of a window, taking a shower or walking, and you get that feeling of doing nothing and letting your mind drift?

Well, your mind, the brain, is doing a lot when it feels like you're doing nothing.

It's going over your memories, shaping your sense of who you are, imagining possible futures, helping you understand other people, and connecting ideas.

That's your brain's default mode network (DMN).

Scientists conducted brain-imaging experiments in the 1990s and early 2000s to find out which regions of the brain were active during demanding tasks, like solving a math problem or memory exercise.

But to measure that, they needed a so-called baseline. They needed to know the level of brain activity at rest and during demanding tasks.

So, between the tasks, they asked participants to relax, close their eyes, stare at a fixed point on a wall, or try not to think of anything at all.

And they measured the participants' brain activity both when they were solving a task and doing nothing. That's how scientists noticed the brain was active in up- and downtime.

They noticed that when participants started to focus on a new task, after having done nothing, some brain areas lit up and other areas dimmed down. That suggests those areas that dimmed down must have been active while the participant was apparently — physically and mentally — at rest.

This realization eventually led to the discovery of the default mode network or DMN.

The DMN is crucial because it helps your brain make sense of your life and experiences.

It helps you connect and revisit memories, reflect on who you are, imagine what might happen in future scenarios, understand other people, build meaning from experience, and connect ideas in new ways.

It turns all your memories, experiences, knowledge, emotions and social information into your internal story. Think of that story as a constantly evolving, updated model of who you are and how you engage with the world around you.

But it's important to understand the DMN involves more than one area of the brain. It is a group of brain regions that light up when you're not focused on an external task.

There's another player in your brain that acts as a monitoring system. You can think of it as constantly asking "Is there anything that needs my attention?"

Imagine you're daydreaming while driving, and a car suddenly swerves into your lane.

In a fraction of a second, your daydream disappears and your attention snaps back to the road. That player is called the salience network (SN).

The salience network helps you notice important signals, like a sound, a mistake, pain, an emotion, a threat, or something happening in your body, and helps shift the brain toward attention and control systems when action is needed.

The DMN, SN and attention/control systems constantly interact.

And the DMN doesn't just turn off completely with every hard task. Parts of it can also contribute when tasks involve memory, stories, meaning, or other people.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Smartphones have filled many of the small empty moments of our days — waiting for the bus, standing in line, eating alone, lying on the couch. We are constantly looking at our devices.

So, it seems reasonable to ask: are we crowding out those moments of boredom where DMN kicks in?

Maybe, but, so far, there's no strong evidence that scrolling directly affects mental health by reducing DMN time.

What we do know is that problematic smartphone use can increase stress, anxiety, task-switching, and disrupt sleep.

And sleep loss and stress also have clearer evidence for altering DMN-related brain connectivity.

When your mind wanders, it might produce a great new idea.

But it might also replay an embarrassing thing you did five years ago. Again. And again.

In depression, the self-reflection part of the DMN can get caught in repetitive negative loops that are hard to stop — what's known as rumination.

Scientists have also found that Alzheimer's disease targets key DMN areas, particularly those responsible for your autobiographical memory and remembering other aspects of the past. Which may help explain why memory problems are so central to the disease.

Protect your sleep and take stress seriously. Sleep loss and stress have direct evidence for disrupting DMN-related connectivity.

Take real quiet breaks after mentally demanding tasks, like studying. It can even help you memorize facts for your exam.

Try to reframe boredom as this essential, energy-intensive active work in your brain that builds your internal narrative and sense of self, consolidates memories, creates connections and sparks creativity.

Try waiting in line without checking your phone. Look out the window next time you take the bus or take a walk without listening to anything.

You can also try scheduling DMN time. It may be the closest thing to a real brain hack.

An Ancient Virus May Hold the Secret to What Makes the Human Brain Different

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Could a virus that infected chimpanzees millions of years ago help explain how the human brain became different?

Researchers at Lund University have discovered that ancient viral DNA embedded in the chimpanzee genome silences a gene involved in early human brain development. Their study, published in Science Advances, found that the gene, LINC00662, remains active in human brain organoids, where it helps support the development of nerve cells and the connections between them.

The finding offers a way to explore how humans and chimpanzees developed different biological traits despite sharing much of their genetic makeup. Evolution can change how existing genes are used, as well as which genes a species carries.

“The virus we investigated specifically affected chimpanzees. Yet humans and chimpanzees have largely the same genetic makeup. That is why it is important to understand the differences in how genes are regulated. Our findings suggest that one gene, LINC00662, plays a significant role in early human brain development,” says Johan Jakobsson, professor of molecular neurogenetics at Lund University.

The genetic remnant belongs to PTERV1, an endogenous retrovirus. Its DNA became embedded in the chimpanzee genome during an ancient infection and has been inherited ever since. The researchers found copies of the viral sequence at numerous locations throughout chimpanzee DNA.

Those sequences are systematically shut down through methylation, a chemical process that helps regulate gene activity. At the LINC00662 location, the inherited viral insertion also keeps the neighboring gene silent.

To examine this difference during brain development, the team grew brain organoids from human and chimpanzee stem cells. These miniature, three-dimensional structures resemble brain tissue and reproduce some features of its early development.

“The gene was strongly expressed in the human organoids, but not at all in the chimpanzee organoids,” says Patricia Gerdes, a postdoctoral researcher in molecular neurogenetics at Lund University.

The researchers then tested whether the viral sequence caused the shutdown. Using the CRISPR-Cas9 gene-editing tool, they removed it from chimpanzee cells. LINC00662 became active again.

“This shows that it is in fact the viral insertion that is responsible for the gene being switched off in chimpanzees,” says Patricia Gerdes.

A separate experiment explored the gene’s role in humans. When the team used CRISPR to silence LINC00662 in human brain organoids, genes involved in nerve cell development and the formation of connections between those cells became less active.

For Jakobsson, the results show how an infection can leave a lasting genetic change across a population, while helping explain how gene regulation can protect the brain or contribute to damage. The team will next investigate whether other viral remnants influenced brain evolution and whether such sequences are linked to brain diseases.

Reference: “Retroviral insertions contributed to the divergence of human and chimpanzee brains” by Patricia Gerdes, Ofelia Karlsson, Raquel Garza, Diahann A. M. Atacho, PingHsun Hsieh, Bharat Prajapati, DongAhn Yoo, Chandramouli Muralidharan, Anita Adami, Carrie Davis-Hansson, Emily M. Johansson, Yogita Sharma, Laura Castilla-Vallmanya, Wiktor West, Meghna Vinod, Lucian Domitrovic, Pia A. Johansson, Jenny Johansson, Christopher H. Douse, Chandrasekhar Kanduri, Patric Jern, Evan E. Eichler and Johan Jakobsson, 23 October 2026, Science Advances. DOI: 10.1126/sciadv.aeg5791

This study was funded by the Swedish Research Council; the Swedish Brain Foundation; Cancerfonden; Barncancerfonden; the Swedish Government Initiative for Strategic Research Areas (MultiPark & StemTherapy); and the European Union’s Horizon Europe research and innovation program.

Stephen Miller Into the Breach: How Susie Wiles’s Failures Portend Miller’s Future

📅 Nov 10, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

There’s a time-honored tradition for new White House chiefs of staff: The outgoing chief invites former holders of the office, Republicans and Democrats, to a get-together (usually in the West Wing), where the newcomer is offered advice. But Donald Trump’s new chief, Stephen Miller—appointed in a jaw-dropping Truth Social post on Friday—won’t be getting a party. First, because the tradition applies only to a president’s first chief. Second, because no Democratic chief would likely want to be caught dead with Miller, a longtime basher of the opposition party. Shortly after hearing that Trump had picked the hard-right firebrand as his new chief, James Carville sent me a text that summed up the prevailing attitude: “This is like Caligula’s appointment of his horse as consul.”

It was different when Susie Wiles came to town. On the evening of November 19, 2024, she was the guest of honor at a dinner party thrown by Jeffrey Zients, Joe Biden’s outgoing White House chief of staff. They were joined by seven of Zients’s predecessors. It was an occasion for bipartisan bonhomie, a tradition started in 2008 by George W. Bush’s chief Joshua Bolten. In a spirit of goodwill and generosity, Bolten had thrown a gathering in his West Wing office for Barack Obama’s incoming chief, Rahm Emanuel. The informal meetup was intended as a way to convey the chiefs’ collective wisdom and experience—and to offer, in miniature, a nuts-and-bolts version of the transition of executive power that is a hallmark of American democracy.

Now evicted from her West Wing corner office, with the president’s agenda in tatters, Wiles has learned that fealty to Trump almost always ends badly.

On the night of the dinner for Wiles, however, a cloud hung over the occasion. Virtually no one at the table thought she could succeed as Donald Trump’s chief of staff.

For almost two years, Wiles was the second most powerful person in the West Wing. Had Trump been interested in governing, she might have helped him solidify a GOP majority in Congress for years to come. But she was unwilling, or unable, to save Trump from himself. On Wiles’s watch, he squandered his second term with a reign of revenge, retribution, and recklessness. Now evicted from her West Wing corner office, with the president’s agenda in tatters, Wiles has learned that fealty to Trump almost always ends badly.

And if past experience is any guide, Wiles’s replacement, Stephen Miller, may well crash and burn in even more spectacular fashion.

There was no one the president relied on or trusted more than Wiles. From their first meeting in 2015 at Trump Tower to her impressive stint as his winning 2024 presidential campaign co-manager, the “ice maiden” had Trump’s back. And he had hers—until last Tuesday’s epic Democratic blowout. Wiles would resign two days later. Indeed, the Republicans’ midterm apocalypse was an extinction event that not even Wiles could have survived.

Ironically, Trump’s savvy political strategist was the architect of what turned out to be a big blue bloodbath. “We’re going to win the midterms,” Wiles told me boldly when I interviewed her for Vanity Fair just a year ago. Even then, three months before Trump initiated a war on Iran, that was a ridiculously rosy prediction. The president’s approval rating was in the gutter. He’d failed to address, much less solve, voters’ primary concerns: affordability, inflation, health care, and housing—and had still not released the remaining Epstein files. How could a battle-hardened political pro like Wiles be so starry-eyed? The answer, I believe, is that in the fog of MAGA-land, she’d lost touch with the real world.

In almost every presidency, the West Wing is a bubble where like-minded acolytes engage in groupthink. But in Trump’s West Wing this phenomenon is exponentially magnified. It’s a parallel universe, one in which the 2020 election was stolen, violent insurrectionists are “patriots,” and the prosecution of political enemies—with scant evidence—is a presidential perk. (“Who would blame him?” Wiles said to me of Trump’s campaign to settle scores.) That, to my mind, is why Wiles seemed so stunned by the firestorm that erupted when her words appeared in my 10,000-word Vanity Fair series last December. She had no idea they would land like bombs on planet Earth.

Wiles invested Trump with almost otherworldly powers. He would lead the GOP to victory in the 2026 midterms, she believed, because of his preternatural ability to rally his base. That’s why, on the eve of the midterms, Wiles was all-in on sending Trump almost everywhere all at once, admonishing people to “vote like I’m on the ballot.” The trouble was voters saw all too clearly what she apparently couldn’t see: Trump was deeply unpopular; on his watch, the country was coming apart.

A year ago, when Wiles made her electoral prediction, I challenged her. “I admire your confidence about the midterms,” I said, “but the stakes are huge, right? And history is not encouraging. Incumbents do stuff and there’s a reaction. And usually, as Obama said, you get shellacked.”

Wiles seemed to shrug off that possibility. “Can you ever imagine Donald Trump saying that?” she said. “No, no.”

Wiles was right. Trump could never say such a thing—because he could never accept the fact that his electoral magic had waned. Neither, it seemed, could Wiles. But as his chief of staff and top political strategist, she was paid to know better.

Even before the election debacle, Trump had reportedly started privately inquiring about her on phone calls, although he lashed out at accounts of such purported conversations. Then, once the carnage sunk in, the die was cast. Wiles offered her resignation. And Trump accepted it.

At the start of her West Wing tenure, I believed Wiles stood a chance of succeeding. If Trump would listen to anyone, I thought, it was the loyal, organized, no-nonsense Wiles. Yet during 10 conversations with her over 10 months, I watched as she abdicated the most important duty of the White House chief: telling the president hard truths. Wiles told me she’d read my book, The Gatekeepers: How the White House Chiefs of Staff Define Every Presidency, and she understood the importance of heeding Reagan chief James Baker’s advice. “You do not serve your president well if you are just a yes man,” he said. In a phone call, she told me, Baker “reminded me that my job wasn’t to be a fangirl.” I asked Wiles, “Have you had any gut check moments where you had to tell the president something he didn’t want to hear?” She paused. “I probably have one a day,” she said. “But they’re over little things, not big.”

Wiles should have known that the midterms would spell disaster. She carried around a list of her priorities, on an index card. Number five: “Don’t forget about organized labor. Don’t forget about young Black men and women and Hispanics…because that’s the future of the country and the backbone of his support. And if we don’t convert those people to being enthusiastic Republicans, then if the Democrats were even just a little bit better, they might win an election.”

Miller is a zealot, a hard-boiled ideologue—an advocate of Trump’s notorious first-term Muslim ban, the brutal paramilitary tactics of ICE, the deployment of the National Guard to Democrat-run cities, and the lethal air strikes on boats in the Caribbean.

They might—and they did. And now Wiles is out, taking up shop, so Trump himself has said, within his political operation. Last November she hinted that she knew this day might come: “You go to bed at night, you say your prayers, you take stock of what the day was and what your day is tomorrow, and you get up and do it again…. So as long as that’s the case and the president’s pleased…” Wiles paused. “He could fire you tomorrow.”

She’s sensitive about her legacy as the first female White House chief. “I’m not an enabler,” she insisted. “I’m also, like, not a bitch. I try to be thoughtful about what I engage in, and what I disagree [with the president] about. I guess time will tell whether I’ve been effective.”

What about Wiles’s successor, Stephen Miller?

Miller is the antithesis of Ronald Reagan’s chief James Baker, a cool pragmatist who could push back on the president’s half-baked ideas. Miller, by contrast, is a zealot, a hard-boiled ideologue—an advocate of Trump’s notorious first-term Muslim ban, the brutal paramilitary tactics of ICE, the deployment of the National Guard to Democrat-run cities, and the lethal air strikes on boats in the Caribbean. He believes in a Hobbesian world, he has stated, “that is governed by strength, that is governed by force, that is governed by power.” Now he’ll be the last person in the room when Trump makes life-and-death decisions about law and order and war and peace.

The man who decreed that the president’s powers “are very substantial and will not be questioned” is now the official charged with questioning the president’s decisions. It’s hard to imagine Miller doing that without a personality transplant. He’s already alienated the House minority leader, and soon to be Speaker, Hakeem Jeffries, who has called Miller “a dangerous xenophobe and reckless extremist who has American blood on his hands,” insisting, “I’ve got zero interest in working with that kind of individual.”

At the White House last November, after a Vanity Fair photo session, I spoke with Miller at length about the midterms, still a year away; about his portfolio in the Trump administration; and about the role of the White House chief of staff. Perched on a chair in the Roosevelt Room, he eyed me warily. He seemed impatient to get back to work. “I understand that your need for me is quite limited, right?” he said.

I asked Miller about his interactions with the president—specifically an Oval Office meeting in which he and Trump reportedly talked about blowing up boats in the Caribbean. “I would never talk about…whether a meeting occurred or didn’t occur,” he said, “or whether it was in a meeting that did occur, or was not in that meeting if it occurred, which I would never say either way.”

Miller was more forthcoming about Wiles’s prediction—that Trump and the GOP would triumph at the polls in 2026. “What about you?” I asked him. “I am confident that we’ll win the midterms,” he replied. History might be against them, he said, “but we have every expectation that we’re going to defy that history.” “Why?” I asked. Miller replied, “Because of the president’s unprecedentedly successful first nine months in office.”

What followed was a black-is-white, up-is-down litany of Trump’s accomplishments, a revisionist history that might have made Karoline Leavitt, another world-class propagandist, blush. “In no particular order,” Miller said, “the taming and defeating of inflation. As you know, we inherited record double-digit inflation and the president made his first priority to bring down the cost of living…cutting the price of energy by high double digits. For many Americans, gas prices have been cut in half since we took over.” None of this, of course, has proved true—but Miller was just warming up.

“And the passage of the largest package of tax cuts and reforms in American history in the form of the One Big Beautiful Bill. Fully securing the Southern border, the most secure border in history, zero illegal crossings, four months in a row. And also the largest number of deportations in American history…. The president has…achieved more in foreign policy than any president in our lifetimes, ending eight wars in eight months—which includes, but is not limited to, securing the historic peace deal between Israel and Gaza, Thailand and Cambodia, Congo and Rwanda, Azerbaijan and Armenia, Israel and Iran, and so on…”

Miller still wasn’t done. The president, he said, “has executed on every promise he’s made to the American people and that includes also taking back our culture and fighting against whether you call it radical leftism or woke or Marxism—a lot of people just call it plain crazy—but taking back our culture and restoring sanity to American life.

“It is my extraordinary privilege and blessing,” Miller added, “to be able to work for the president of the United States, to implement an agenda that I believe in wholly and completely and with every fiber of my being.”

I was there, of course, because I was writing a story on Susie Wiles and her West Wing team. I asked him, point-blank: “Would you agree, though, that it’s also the chief of staff’s job to tell the president what he doesn’t want to hear?”

“No, I wouldn’t say that,” Miller replied, finding fault with my framing of the question and insisting that this president wants to hear everything. “The chief of staff would never have to encounter the scenario you’re describing.” Miller said. “President Trump craves, he loves the every-minute detail of management, having every piece of information, having every side of everything, having inputs into the Oval Office from every square, every quarter, every person.”

As for the president’s staff, Miller said, their job is “implementing the president’s vision, the president’s directions, the president’s agenda, the president’s plans for the future…. We’re just here on his behalf to see that that is done.”

As the great political columnist Molly Ivins once wrote, “It probably sounded better in the original German.”

Chris Whipple is a contributing writer at Vanity Fair and a Fall 2026 Resident Fellow at Harvard’s Kennedy School Institute of Politics. You can follow him on Substack.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/providers/kaiser-permanente-grows-revenue-9-doubles-operating-margin-q3-2026" hreflang="en">Kaiser Permanente grows revenue 9%, doubles operating margin in Q3 2026</a>

📅 2026-11-10  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
Kaiser Permanente grows revenue 9%, doubles operating margin in Q3 2026" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

In this year’s third quarter, Kaiser Permanente grew its operating revenues by 9% and more than doubled its operating income compared to the year prior, the organization recently shared.

The country’s largest nonprofit health system and its subsidiaries logged $34.7 billion in operating revenues and $34.2 billion in operating expenses for the three months ended Sept. 30, 2026, according to a release outlining topline financial numbers.

Its operating income was $495 million (1.4% operating margin), as compared to the prior year’s $218 million (0.7% operating margin). That’s a bit tighter than the system reported in 2026’s earlier quarters, though the organization often notes that its margins tend to thin out as the year progresses due to health plan enrollment cycles and seasonal expense increases.

Beyond its recently announced plans to add Oklahoma system Integris Health to Risant Health, its subsidiary value-based care platform, the organization said that widespread affordability concerns are fueling a focus on operational efficiencies and driving its investments in value-based care.

CEO Greg Adams said in the release that Kaiser’s rates “are 12% to 18% lower than our competitors’, on average, when comparing similar products. We’re advancing value-based care by expanding access to health management resources and coordinating care around measurable outcomes, not volume. Our employees and physicians deliver better health outcomes every day, and I’m grateful for their commitment to our members and patients.”

Kaiser and Risant’s capital spending during the quarter was $1.2 billion, down slightly from last year’s $1.3 billion, and spans more than five dozen projects in active design and construction “including seven major hospital construction projects.”

“We made progress on infrastructure projects to comply with hospital seismic regulations,” Executive Vice President and Chief Financial Officer Kathy Lancaster said in the release. “We also continued to invest in the facilities and technology members need to receive care in the setting they prefer, including inpatient, ambulatory and telehealth services.”

Kaiser said it collected $1.8 billion in investment and other income during the most recent quarter, down from almost $2.4 billion it reported in Q3 2025. That gap left its net income at about $2.3 billion, below the prior year’s $2.6 billion.

Kaiser and Risant’s membership was almost 13.4 million as of the end of the quarter, on par with the end of Q2. The system also outlined “nearly $1.0 billion in charitable grants, loans and investments to strengthen the healthcare infrastructure of communities” that included about $315 million in financial assistance and charity care.

Kaiser Permanente reported $127.7 billion of operating revenue in 2025 and has been growing fast with affiliate acquisitions through Risant Health, helping fuel a 44% jump in revenue since 2020. Last year, it logged $1.4 billion in operating income (1.1% operating margin) across 12 months, as well as a $9.3 billion in net income and $4.8 billion of capital spending.

The organization’s quarterly financial update comes ahead of a more granular regulatory disclosure, typically filed within a few additional weeks. That document could include additional information about its plans for Risant to absorb $3.3 billion, Oklahoma City-based Integris Health, which comprises 10 acute and 10 specialty hospitals. That deal is expected to close early next year, though its announcement was short on any financial details.

Is Your Sodium Intake Impacting Your Hiking Performance? A Dietitian Explains Why It Matters.

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Take a look at the nutrition label on a freeze-dried backpacking meal, and you might be surprised by how much sodium it contains. Some meals pack more than 1,500 mg into a single pouch, roughly two-thirds of the recommended daily limit of 2,300 mg for most adults. That’s a lot for one dinner, but when you’re spending hours sweating your way up steep climbs, you’re also losing sodium along with water.

Sodium helps regulate fluid balance and supports normal nerve and muscle function. Yet there’s a contradiction in how you might think about it on the trail: you question the sodium in your freeze-dried dinner while adding electrolyte powder to your water bottle and snacking on pretzels, jerky, and cheese.

So, are you consuming too much sodium or not enough? The answer depends on what’s already in your food, how much you’re sweating, and how you’re replacing fluids.

You can pack nearly identical amounts of food and end up with dramatically different sodium intakes. I compared two realistic days of backpacking food, each containing commercial meals, protein bars, snacks, and other familiar trail staples. Both provide roughly 3,150 calories and weigh about 23 ounces dry, yet one contains 5,465 mg of sodium while the other contains just 1,975 mg.

That’s nearly 3,500 mg of difference before you add a single electrolyte packet. Some of the variation comes down to individual products: Mountain House Beef Lasagna contains 1,180 mg of sodium, compared with 340 mg in Good To-Go Pad Thai. Even switching pretzel flavors or protein bar brands can make a noticeable difference. Neither food bag is automatically better, but the comparison shows why it’s worth checking what you’re already carrying before deciding to supplement.

Health experts recommend limiting sodium for a reason. Consistently consuming more than your body needs can raise blood pressure, increasing your risk of heart disease and stroke over time. A particularly salty meal can also leave you thirsty or temporarily bloated as your body regulates its fluid balance. If you’ve been advised to limit sodium because of high blood pressure, kidney disease, or another medical condition, those recommendations still matter when you’re backpacking.

But eating a high-sodium diet day after day isn’t necessarily the same as eating salty meals during a strenuous backpacking trip. When you’re sweating heavily, you lose sodium you wouldn’t lose while sitting at a desk. That doesn’t mean every milligram of sodium in your food is necessary, or that you should add electrolytes without thinking about what you’re already eating. It simply means your activity and conditions matter when interpreting those numbers on the label.

Even if you’re hiking alongside someone on the same trail, you may be losing very different amounts of sodium. Sweat losses depend on two things: how much you sweat and how concentrated that sweat is. Research has found sweat sodium concentrations ranging from roughly 230 to 1,600 mg per liter. Temperature, exertion, clothing, heat acclimatization, and individual physiology all affect your losses, which means mileage alone tells you very little about how much sodium you’ve lost.

Here’s another wrinkle: losing 800 mg of sodium through sweat doesn’t automatically mean you need to consume 800 mg to replace it. Your body regulates sodium and water balance, and research suggests targeted sodium replacement matters more under certain combinations of prolonged exercise, substantial sweat losses, and fluid replacement. That’s why taking an electrolyte packet every hour isn’t necessarily appropriate. A packet doesn’t account for how much you’re sweating, how much you’re drinking, or how much sodium you’re getting from food.

Sodium doesn’t have to come from an electrolyte supplement to count. Salty snacks, meals, and electrolyte drinks all contribute to your intake, although when you consume that sodium can matter during long, sweaty days. Imagine spending ten hours hiking in warm weather, sweating heavily and drinking water but eating very little. At camp, you prepare a freeze-dried dinner containing 1,500 mg of sodium. That sodium helps replace losses and retain fluids during recovery, but it wasn’t available during the preceding hours of hiking.

Eating salty snacks throughout a prolonged, sweaty hike provides some sodium along the way instead of waiting until dinner. That doesn’t mean you need electrolyte powder in every water bottle. If you’re regularly eating pretzels, cheese, jerky, or other salty foods, you’re already consuming sodium during the day. On the other hand, if your trail menu contains relatively little sodium or you struggle to eat while hiking, an electrolyte drink may be a convenient alternative. What matters isn’t whether the sodium comes from a packet or a snack, but whether you’re adding something your food isn’t already providing.

It’s easy to assume that replacing the sodium you lose through sweat will protect you from exercise-associated hyponatremia (EAH), a potentially dangerous drop in blood sodium. But according to the Wilderness Medical Society, most cases occur primarily because you drink too much fluid relative to your losses, often combined with your body’s tendency to retain water during prolonged exercise. Sweat sodium losses can contribute under some circumstances, but taking electrolyte supplements won’t prevent hyponatremia if you’re overdrinking.

The guidelines recommend avoiding overdrinking, generally by drinking according to thirst or using fluid needs you’ve estimated from experience in similar conditions. They also recommend having salty foods or drinks available during long, hot activities. Headaches, fatigue, nausea, and muscle cramps aren’t reliable indicators that you need more sodium because they can have several causes. If you or a hiking partner develops worsening confusion, repeated vomiting, seizures, or collapses during or after prolonged exertion, seek urgent medical help rather than assuming more water or electrolytes will solve the problem.

There’s no simple answer. Your sweat losses vary with the conditions and your individual physiology. Even knowing exactly how much sodium you lose wouldn’t tell you how much to replace. For context, 1,500 mg per day is considered an adequate intake for most adults under ordinary conditions, while 2,300 mg is the general recommended daily limit. Neither is a backpacking-specific target. A food bag containing 5,465 mg isn’t automatically excessive for a strenuous hiking day, just as one containing 1,975 mg isn’t necessarily inadequate. And since research hasn’t consistently shown that extra sodium improves endurance performance, there’s little reason to chase a precise replacement target.

The bottom line? Hike your own hike. If you’re curious about your sodium intake, track what you eat and drink, the conditions you’re hiking in, and how you feel during and afterward. You might notice patterns that help you plan future trips, without getting caught up in replacing every milligram of sodium you sweat out.

Want more Outside health stories? Sign up for the Bodywork newsletter. And for tips on how to train, fuel, and recover better, sign up for the Sweat Science 101 newsletter.

Hidden Behind the Badge

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 9, 2026 | Reviewed by Monica Vilhauer Ph.D.

Co-authored by Dr. Jessica Muccione and Dr. Michael Pittaro

Every occupation can take a toll on workers’ mental health, but some professions are considered more stressful than others due to the nature of work-related exposures. To date, policing remains one of the most stressful professions. Hidden behind every badge is a law enforcement professional trying their very best to navigate a steady, and at times relentless, stream of psychological and emotional stress that makes resilience a key attribute that is essential for survival where suicide rates exceed those of the average citizen. Most, if not all, law enforcement officers will, at some point in their careers, be faced with stressful, traumatic encounters that quite literally become embedded in their psyche, and if not addressed, will likely worsen.

This topic has been extensively studied, but most studies focus exclusively on male police officers; yet, emerging research suggests that female officers, in particular, often experience these challenges in distinct and deeply personal ways that differ from the experiences of male officers. Exploring these differences can help agencies design wellness initiatives that truly support all who serve by recognizing and adjusting for gender differences. Although the percentage of female officers pursuing a career in law enforcement has been steadily increasing, female officers only comprise 13.8% of all US law enforcement officers; that is likely why their unique circumstances have been largely understudied and, for the most part, overlooked within the research and practitioner literature (Statista, 2024).

Law enforcement officers are repeatedly exposed to traumatic incidents, most of which are attributed to operational stress. Operational stress is commonly tied to the trauma that police officers encounter in the course of their work, such as responding to homicide scenes, domestic and sexual assaults, fatal collisions, suicides, and other acts of violence that can have a profound influence on officers, leading to anxiety, depression, sleep disturbances, PTSD, and in some extreme cases, suicide (Bishopp et al., 2019). To compound matters, today’s law enforcement officers are also faced with staff shortages, mandatory overtime, erratic shifts, insufficient pay, public scrutiny, and at times, unsupportive, ineffective leadership. In fact, Violanti et al. (2017) found that female police officers report a roughly 37% higher prevalence of lack of support from supervisors compared to male officers.

The paucity of research to date suggests that female officers are more likely to internalize the emotional impact of stressors that lead to burnout when compared to male officers (Rabichuk, Williamson, & Frankel, 2025). In law enforcement, female officers often place significant pressure on themselves. There is a constant burden to maintain a professional image and avoid appearing vulnerable or weak. In response, female officers tend to suppress emotions rather than seek help because they are persistently compelled to prove their competence in a historically male-dominated field, contributing to the high likelihood of chronic stress and burnout.

Female officers are challenged with unique workplace stressors that extend beyond the basic demands of police work, some of which male officers cannot relate to, and as a result, can adversely affect performance, well-being, retention, and career advancement. Some of these stressors include, but are not limited to, pregnancy, postpartum recovery, menopause, and the demands of shift work. It should come as no surprise that contemporary research suggests that police leaders who understand these realities are better positioned to implement policies that support female officer wellness while strengthening organizational effectiveness.

Pregnancy presents unique challenges for women in law enforcement, particularly when agency policies regarding modified assignments and accommodations vary widely. Research suggests female officers may experience elevated reproductive health risks, including miscarriage, highlighting the need for evidence-based workplace policies, reproductive health education, flexible assignments, and supportive supervision.

Illias, Demou, and Riach (2025) emphasized that returning to work after childbirth presents another set of challenges unique to female law enforcement officers. Recovery from childbirth, sleep disruptions, breastfeeding, and childcare responsibilities often lead to concerns about job security and future career advancement. Female officers who received little to no agency support upon return to work experienced significantly higher rates of depression and anxiety than those who had the support of their agencies (Illias et al., 2025). It is therefore recommended that police leaders create family-friendly policies, comprehensive return-to-work policies and programs, breastfeeding spaces, and other supportive accommodations.

This is undoubtedly one of the least studied issues affecting female police officers. Hofman and Spiegel (2026) conducted a qualitative study in which the study’s participants (female police officers) reported concerns about stigma, perceptions of reduced competence, and a lack of understanding from leadership. For police leadership, menopause should be viewed as a workforce wellness issue as opposed to a personal matter (Hofman & Spiegel, 2026). Educating and training supervisors and fellow officers normalizes conversations about women’s health that are typically shunned.

Understanding the realities that female officers face throughout their policing careers is not simply a gender-equity issue, but rather an organizational issue because agencies that recognize and respond to these challenges are more likely than not to retain female officers, improve wellness outcomes, and strengthen modern-day law enforcement agencies.

Dr. Jessica Muccione began her law enforcement career in 2016 in Luzerne County, Pennsylvania, before transitioning to a police department in Northampton County, Pennsylvania, in 2018, where she continues to serve as a full-time police officer. She is also an instructor at the Lackawanna College Police Academy for MPOETC and an Adjunct Professor in the college’s Cybersecurity Program. Dr. Muccione holds a Ph.D. in Criminal Justice, a Master’s Degree in Criminology, a Bachelor’s Degree in Homeland and International Security, and an Associate’s Degree in Criminal Justice. She has earned professional certificates in Law Enforcement Administrative Development from Walden University and Supervision of Police Personnel from Northwestern University’s School of Police Staff & Command. Her published qualitative research examines the impact of male sexual offender recidivism on sex crime investigations and is available at https://scholarworks.waldenu.edu/dissertations/14367/.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/providers/advocate-health-increase-total-employee-compensation-846m-2027" hreflang="en">Advocate Health to increase total employee compensation by $846M for 2027</a>

📅 2026-11-09  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
Advocate Health to increase total employee compensation by $846M for 2027" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Nonprofit health system Advocate Health will invest an additional $1.24 billion in its workforce beginning in 2027, with approximately $846 million dedicated towards compensation and $390 million towards incremental benefits.

Advocate will increase its minimum wage to $20 an hour, building on its $18.85 per hour minimum wage established this year.

For a 40-hour workweek, that wage would pay over $26,520 more per year than the federal minimum wage, it said.

Compensation investments will also include merit increases, incentives, pay-range updates and other investments in pay, executives said in the Nov. 5 announcement.

“The most important investment we make isn’t in buildings, technology or equipment. It’s in our people,” said Eugene A. Woods, Advocate Health CEO, in a statement. “Healthcare is a 24/7 calling and every single day our 170,000 teammates bring extraordinary skill, compassion and commitment to the patients and communities we serve. Their dedication is what brings our purpose to life for the millions of people who count on us each year.”

The country’s third-largest nonprofit health system currently covers approximately 83% of employee medical costs, and plans to invest an additional $138 million for what it says will absorb “more than 90% of the projected $149 million increase in medical plan costs.”

Moreover, the health system’s 2027 benefits package extends fully paid parental bonding leave to four weeks enterprise-wide; expands paid bereavement leave; continues coverage for GLP-1 medications for weight loss; provides no-cost mental health support for employees and family members; continues sustained retirement contributions of up to 6% of eligible pay and offers education assistance across more than 1,300 eligible programs.

The package also offers student loan repayment assistance, with with lifetime support of up to $21,000 for eligible full-time employees and $10,500 for eligible part-time employees.

“The benefits that matter most are the ones that make a real difference in people’s lives,” said Nakesha Lopez, Advocate Health senior executive vice president and chief people and culture officer, in a statement. “We want our benefits to reflect what teammates need at different stages of their lives, while keeping affordability at the forefront and providing meaningful support for their health, families and financial futures.”

Plant-Based Milk Alternatives May Not Give You Enough Iodine. Here’s Why That Matters

📅 Nov 09, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Replacing dairy with plant-based alternatives (PBAs), even partially, could substantially lower iodine intake, especially if those alternatives aren’t fortified, according to a recent study published in the Lancet Planetary Health.

The United Kingdom-based researchers found that only 12% of UK plant-based milk, yogurt, and cheese products analyzed were fortified with iodine. This compares with 61% for calcium and 46% for vitamin B12.

In the United States, the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) provides guidance on the nutritional content of PBAs, but fortification with iodine is voluntary.

The study found that replacing as little as 20% of dairy intake with plant-based alternatives could increase the risk of inadequate iodine intake, particularly among adolescent girls and women of reproductive age.

“People often worry about a decline in calcium intake when they replace some of their dairy with plant-based alternatives. As a result, iodine is frequently overlooked. This study serves as a valuable reminder of iodine’s significance as a nutrient,” said Victoria Finn, MD, board certified endocrinologist at Medical Offices of Manhattan, who wasn’t involved in the study.

The study included analysis of UK plant-based milk, cheese, and yogurt products, as well as dietary data from 3,500 people.

The researchers used statistical modeling to predict how swapping dairy for PBAs might affect iodine and other nutrients.

The people who are most vulnerable to iodine inadequacy are adolescent females and people at reproductive age, especially those who are pregnant or who plan to become pregnant.

The researchers estimated that replacing 20% to 35% of dairy intake with PBAs would increase the proportion of people with inadequate iodine intake:

When the researchers modeled the replacement of all dairy products with PBAs, the proportion of adolescent females with inadequate iodine intake rose to 80%. Among females ages 19 to 49, that proportion remained relatively stable as replacement rates increased.

It is important to keep in mind that the study used dietary modeling rather than direct measurement of iodine status.

Iodine is important for thyroid hormone production, metabolism, and both fetal and child brain development.

“Your thyroid gland requires iodine to produce thyroid hormones, which play a crucial role in regulating metabolism and supporting normal growth and development,” said Finn.

She added that if you consistently consume too little iodine over an extended period, the thyroid gland may enlarge and eventually struggle to produce enough hormones. This condition is known as hypothyroidism, which can lead to various symptoms such as lethargy, sleepiness, weight gain, dry skin, and more.

“Hypothyroidism can be particularly dangerous during pregnancy, as the baby’s brain, nervous system, and organs rely heavily on the mother’s thyroid hormone during the early stages of development,” Finn added.

In the United Kingdom, dairy is a major iodine source due to cattle feed supplementation, not because dairy is naturally high in iodine.

In the United States, dairy is also an important source of iodine for the same reasons.

However, the risk profile for iodine deficiency in the U.S. may differ from the U.K., as iodized salt is also a significant source of iodine and is widely available in the U.S., but not in the U.K.

According to the National Institutes of Health (NIH), the recommended daily intake of iodine depends on your age.

Plant-based or plant-forward diets have grown in popularity, in part due to the health benefits they offer. For example, consuming less meat can reduce your risk of heart disease, type 2 diabetes, obesity, and high cholesterol.

However, as this study suggests, it may be possible to reduce your iodine intake when transitioning to a plant-based diet. There are ways you can protect your iodine intake when choosing a more plant-forward diet, though.

Check the labels on plant-based dairy alternatives to find those fortified with iodine. You can also read the nutrition label. Iodine may be listed as potassium iodide or potassium iodate.

Seafood, eggs, and iodized salt are all good sources of iodine.

You can also discuss iodine supplements with your healthcare professional.

“Iodine supplements should be taken with caution, especially without checking your thyroid levels,” said Finn. “Additionally, be cautious with kelp supplements, as their iodine content can vary, and excessive iodine intake can disrupt thyroid function.”
