Survival after sudden cardiac arrest didn’t improve despite training bystanders, emergency responders

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Test your knowledge with our new weekday mini crossword

Liz covers cardiovascular and metabolic disease, asking why we’re not doing better or even losing ground against the world’s leading killer. Send tips on Signal at LizC.22.

CHICAGO — Sudden cardiac arrest statistics are chilling. In the United States, 350,000 people’s heartbeats, breathing, pulse, and blood circulation will all halt outside hospitals this year, seemingly out of the blue. These are not heart attacks. Only 1 in 10 people will survive them.

CPR and defibrillation can save their lives (and brains), but that depends on bystanders and emergency response systems being both nearby and prepared to step in — immediately. Survival chances drop by 10% for every minute that goes by without CPR.

Hints from observational studies suggested better, more comprehensive community training can help, which means educating 911 dispatchers, first responders, and potential bystanders. But results from a randomized controlled trial presented Sunday at the American Heart Association’s scientific sessions and published in the New England Journal of Medicine on Sunday show that approach has not worked, or at least not yet. But examples in Denmark may point the way forward.

“We performed an efficient, large, and high-quality trial engaging large segments of the community in emergency response, and in spite of this, survival remained low and unchanged,” Carolina Malta Hansen, the study’s lead author, said at a media briefing. “A multifaceted community and EMS implementation strategy did not improve survival with a favorable neurologic outcome for patients with out-of-hospital cardiac arrest. However, in the final year, there appeared to be an improvement in bystanders.”

Starting in 2021, researchers randomly assigned participants in 60 counties in North Carolina to receive intensive education on cardiac arrest intervention or to continue with standard practice. The intervention entailed training 911 dispatchers to improve their rapid recognition of cardiac arrest and better guide bystanders through CPR. First responders were educated on the use of automated external defibrillators before emergency services arrived. Community members in workplaces, religious groups, government offices, and other settings received training in CPR.

About 8.5 million people live in these counties. During the study’s five years, 22,459 adult residents experienced cardiac arrest outside the hospital. Their median age was 64; 37% were women, 33% were Black; and 29% lived in rural areas.

The numbers were split this way: 11,615 people experienced out-of-hospital cardiac arrest in counties that received intensive education, and 10,844 people had out-of-hospital cardiac arrest in communities that did not receive the intervention.

Amrish Deshmukh, a cardiac electrophysiologist who treats patients with heart rhythm disorders at the University of Michigan Health Frankel Cardiovascular Center, called the study results disappointing, but not surprising.

“Many interventions that have been studied in out-of-hospital cardiac arrest and in post-arrest care unfortunately have not been shown to improve survival or neurologic outcomes,” he told STAT in an email. He was not involved in the new trial. “Prior studies have shown that mobile phone alerts, volunteer training, and increased automated external defibrillator (AEDs) availability can increase the performance of CPR and use of AEDs, but not all have shown improvements in survival.”

And yet, study author Malta Hansen, a cardiologist and clinical research associate professor at Herlev and Gentofte Hospital and Copenhagen EMS in Denmark, is hopeful. In the trial’s fourth year, an exploratory analysis showed the percentage of patients who underwent CPR performed by a bystander increased, suggesting that this component of the intervention was effective. More time may be needed to see which elements mattered and whether they affect survival.

“In light of the apparent improvement in CPR for the last year, further evaluation in the longer, more intense intervention is important,” she said.

Malta Hansen cites the case of Denmark, where rates of bystander CPR and survival are much higher than in the United States. The North Carolina study trained almost 5% of the population, at which point researchers started seeing a difference in delivery of CPR between the group that received training and the group that did not.

“Obviously, that’s not enough. We have observational evidence from other countries like Denmark, where 80% of the population is trained and 75% of patients receive high-speed care,” she said.

In Denmark, the defibrillation rate is 11%, compared to 2% in the U.S. In Denmark, survival is about 16%, compared to 10% in the U.S.

That CPR-trained rate in Denmark climbed from 20% in the early 2000s to 80% now, Malta Hansen said. “It’s not something that’s done in two or three years.”

Here’s what helps: To get your driver’s license in Denmark, you have to show that you have certification in CPR. “It’s just an incredibly efficient way to reach everybody,” she said.

Brahmajee Nallamothu, an interventional cardiologist and editor in chief of the AHA journal Circulation: Cardiovascular Quality and Outcomes, had another theory on timing. Starting the trial right as Covid-19 was emerging could have affected educational efforts and could explain why effects of the training were delayed. He also asked if the educational intervention was the right approach.

“I don’t think anyone would argue with the importance of early treatment in out-of-hospital cardiac arrest. When we think about bystander CPR, early defibrillation with AEDs, the challenge is: How do you translate that?” Nallamathu, who was not involved in the trial, said. “I think we still struggle with what’s the best way to train individuals so that they have sustained ability to understand and then utilize that tool when that situation arises, that’s an area that’s still complicated and still unknown.”

Malta Hansen’s team will follow this population in North Carolina for at least another year.

“We have established a fantastic infrastructure and network and collaboration, and everybody’s very excited to keep working on this,” she said. “What we need are communities who are very engaged and very eager to keep working on this. So we believe that future efforts to guide us will be successful.”

That won’t be easy, Michigan’s Deshmukh said.

“Implementation science is challenging (as evidenced by this and other negative trials), but is what I suspect will move the needle the most,” he wrote.

Coping With an Acute Injury or Health Diagnosis

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 8, 2026 | Reviewed by Jessica Schrader

Many clients who come to see me in therapy are coping with an acute injury or health change. For example, they may have sustained an injury while playing a sport or been given a new medical diagnosis. A sudden injury or health change can feel shocking and is often difficult to cope with. You may be trying to adjust to changes in mobility and functioning and worried about when and if you will return to your “normal” self.

Often, the most pressing concern is: Will I get better? And when? The uncertainty is difficult to tolerate. While some injuries or diagnoses have clear treatment plans and timelines, more often it is not crystal clear what someone’s recovery or medical journey will look like. The anxious brain likes to jump to worst-case scenarios when uncertainty exists. It takes a “better safe than sorry” approach, leading us to think about all the possible horrible outcomes so we can be prepared. However, these worst-case scenarios may never come to pass, and anxiety can start to take up more and more space in your daily life.

An injury or new diagnosis can feel like a never-ending emotional roller coaster ride that you never wanted to take. It can be challenging to acknowledge the reality of the injury or diagnosis, including sudden changes you may be facing, without thinking that your life is doomed. Below are some strategies for coping with an acute injury or health diagnosis.

It is helpful to be informed about your condition, as a general understanding can help you have effective conversations with health care providers. It is useful to use reputable sources for gathering information, and your medical team may be able to offer resources. Keep in mind that not all of your questions may have firm answers, and there may be limits to what you or your medical team may be able to know.

While it is difficult to tolerate uncertainty, be mindful of not going down an information-gathering rabbit hole that consumes too much of your time and life. If you notice that trying to gather information about your condition is taking over your daily life or that your anxiety is increasing, set time limits for gathering information. For example, 30 to 60 minutes a week.

A sudden injury or diagnosis can create a wave of emotions. You may feel scared, apprehensive, sad, angry, frustrated, etc. Trying to suppress your emotions can intensify them. Find an authentic way to express how you are feeling. For example, take five to 10 minutes daily to write out your thoughts or feelings, say them aloud on a voice memo app, or share how you are feeling with a trusted friend or family member. If it feels unfamiliar and anxiety-provoking to talk about your feelings, consider doing so in a less focused or pressured environment. For instance, you may talk about your feelings while traveling in the car with soft music playing.

It can be unfamiliar and challenging for some people to accept help from others. Maybe you are used to doing everything yourself, and accepting help leads you to feel incapable. However, accepting help from others allows people who care about you to be of assistance during a difficult time. This is beneficial for them as well as for you. Also, spend time with people you are comfortable with and enjoy. It is natural to feel down, withdraw from others, and isolate ourselves during stressful periods. Yet, isolation can worsen mood. Even if you don’t initially feel like it, carve out some time with others. Your desire to be with others may increase over time. Also, it may be helpful to seek out connection with others who have the same injury or diagnosis. Talking with others with a shared understanding and hearing about others’ treatments, recovery, and coping strategies can feel relieving and supportive.

It can be difficult to accept limitations or the reality that there are some things you may not be able to do or need to do in a different way. It is important to give yourself space and time to grieve any losses you are experiencing. Expecting too much of yourself or trying to ignore the current circumstances can lead to increased frustration, anger, and sadness. Also, trying to meet unrealistic goals may deplete you and lead to less functioning and ability than you would have if you set more realistic and sustainable goals.

Just because you have limitations or there are certain things you cannot do does not mean you are incapable. Non-judgmentally ask yourself what you can and want to realistically take on, and aim to set small, doable goals.

A new injury or diagnosis can be anxiety-provoking and scary, and can highlight what or who you care most about. Certain activities may be different now, yet there are likely things or people who continue to be meaningful to you. Carve out time for them. Stay connected, or reconnect, to what feels most important and fulfilling to you. You may find that many of these things are simple and joyful, such as spending time with loved ones or time in nature.

The anxious part of our minds equates uncertainty with bad based on self-protection and preservation. While a good outcome is not guaranteed, neither is a bad one. The future could be incredibly difficult, and it also could be better than you are thinking or neutral. When your mind wants to leap ahead to worst-case scenarios, pause, take a slow breath, and remind yourself, “Uncertain does not mean bad; it simply means unknown.” Then refocus on the present moment and a values-based action you can take today.

Day 31: The Concert From the Castle

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

With the moon still plump and full, I set out through the mountains from the restful town I’d slept in.

It was daylight, and as I carefully descended the path strewn with loose rocks, I listened to the book that taught me about the Road to Santiago.

In the town before my destination, I planned to stop and eat breakfast. The cafe cats caught my attention as I approached. As the whole street came into view, I saw my Italian brother smiling at me. I ran to him and gave him a hug.

We always managed to stumble upon each other without planning, and I loved it.

When we made it to the city of Ponferrada, we went directly to the donativo albergue—San Nicolás de Flüe Pilgrims Hostel—to wait for it to open. The courtyard was large, with many trees, a large wooden totem, and a vine-covered sitting area. I went into the church nearby looking for a restroom. What I found, though, made me take a seat in the pew.

Within those tiny walls, an extraordinary mural was painted on the ceiling, depicting scenes from the Camino de Santiago, the four cardinal directions, the elements, and the stars. It stirred both the artist and the spiritual side within me.

When I went back to where we were relaxing, our Argentine brother had arrived. The three of us called ourselves “Familia Real”, the name emphasizing the bond we had created.

Once again, without planning, we were all together.

As a religious donativo albergue, I was placed on the woman’s side of the building. When I saw the name of the room I’d been assigned to, I couldn’t help but smile. “Cruz del Ferro.”

That place would forever hold a special place in my heart.

The brothers and I went on a side quest to a bodega overlooking the Castle of the Knights Templar.

After the siesta hours ended, I went to the Castle of the Knights Templar to embark on the tourist part of the day. I’d made it a few feet into the castle walkway when I saw my pilgrim friend below on the street. He was a musician who carried a flute with him everywhere, taking full advantage to play and sing and jam at every opportunity. I yelled down, asking him to play, but that was already his plan.

A man he met in the city joined him with a violin and I was entranced.

Standing on a castle wall, listening to music from artists I knew, with the city and mountains stretching out behind them, felt surreal.

That moment will forever be stamped into my memory.

After each song ended, I clapped from my perch on the castle wall, and the pilgrim smiled, skipping around with pride. His passion for music poured out of him with such a burning fire, and I felt lucky to witness it.

I knew I had a lot more to see, though, so I went on my way.

Exploring the exterior, I went level after level, corridor after corridor. I don’t like big houses, but now I want to live in a castle.

The secret passageways and rooms reminded me of the forts I used to build as a child.

And being on top of that tower overlooking the castle, the city, and the mountains?

I learned more about medieval culture than I ever had in my life. I explored an exhibit of a collection of books. The texts on display were all shapes and sizes, some as thick as my head, some smaller than a deck of cards.

Had it not been for exhaustion hitting me, I could’ve stayed for several more hours.

After 2.5 hours, though, I knew it was time to move on.

A grocery run on my way back to the albergue, a simple dinner in the chaotic kitchen, then quality time with my brothers.

The day had mirrored the life I’d grown accustomed to on the Camino.

In the kitchen at the end of the night, I met a fellow American for the first time. He’d seen me before in a different town and started the conversation with,

“You were working on your laptop and all I could think was ‘damn, she must be writing a book.’”

The latest status symbol of the 1% is a third kid. ‘It has become rarer for everyone else,’ says an economist

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

For decades, the markers of extreme wealth were easy to spot: the second home, the fancy cars, the Ivy League education. New analysis of Federal Reserve data points to a subtler one: a third child.

Mike Konczal, vice president of policy and research at the Economic Security Project and a former White House economist, studied households headed by 45- to 64-year-olds in his analysis of the Fed’s Survey of Consumer Finances, pooling five surveys from 2013 through 2025. He found that 49% of top-1% earners have three or more children, compared with about 40% of the rest of the top 10%.

Families with four or more kids are about as common in both groups, so the gap comes down to the third child: 34% of the 1% stop at exactly three, versus 24% of the merely affluent.

What changed isn’t the rich. Since the 1990s, families with three or more kids have become less common overall and among the rest of the top 10%, while the 1%’s share barely moved. “What’s new is that it has become rarer for everyone else,” Konczal writes.

Big families thin out in the upper middle class. Rank families by wealth instead of income, and among those wealthier than about three-quarters of Americans their age, but well short of the top, only about a third have three or more kids.

That’s well below families further down the wealth ladder. In the top 1%, half do.

Many of these families earn too much for federal child care subsidies, which are capped at 85% of a state’s median income, but not enough to hire their way around the problem.

Elaine Shi and her husband once thought they might have four kids. They stopped at three. Asked how much cost factored in, Shi, the CEO and co-founder of body-composition scanning company BodySpec, said simply: “None.” What settled it was their third child’s “firecracker personality,” along with her health during each pregnancy.

Shi doesn’t see that divide among the people she knows. “Candidly, this has not been my experience,” she says. What stands out to her is timing: “Relatively few of my peers in our late 30s have chosen to have three children because they started having the first one relatively late in life.”

Big families used to be ordinary. In 1976, about two-thirds of mothers in their early 40s had three or more kids; by 2016, 38% did, according to Pew Research Center.

The U.S. fertility rate, close to two children per woman in 1995, hit a record low of 1.57 in 2025, by a Wall Street Journal calculation using federal data.

Americans still say they want more: 42% called three or more kids ideal in Gallup’s July 2025 poll. But more than 7 in 10 say that raising kids is unaffordable for most people, according to BYU’s 2025 American Family Survey.

Shi’s third child arrived at a hectic moment. Her older two, about two years apart, were starting school, and their needs were diverging.

“It didn’t seem fair to drag an infant along to every gymnastics and swim class, nor did it seem fair to forego those kinds of activities to stick to a baby’s nap schedule,” she says. She was also mid-fundraise and needed to get back to work fast. “So we ended up investing in multiple caregivers to better meet everyone’s needs.”

The biggest financial change from two kids to three, she says, was “definitely childcare.”

Most families can’t hire their way out. Full-day care for a child cost $6,552 to $15,600 a year in 2022, or 8.9% to 16% of median family income, according to the Labor Department. And that’s for one child.

By one LendingTree estimate, a two-child household needs to earn $400,000 a year for childcare to be affordable.

A third kid often means a bigger house, too. The typical existing single-family home sold for $412,500 in 2024, about five times the median household income, according to Harvard’s Joint Center for Housing Studies. In the 1990s, it was closer to three times.

The U.S. recorded just over half a million more births than deaths in 2025, and Census Bureau and Congressional Budget Office projections show that cushion ending in the next decade, the Wall Street Journal reported. After that, population growth would depend on immigration.

Economists warn that fewer births mean a smaller future workforce, slower growth and more strain on Social Security.

Konczal cautions that his numbers show a correlation, but it’s the one you’d expect if money solves the hard parts of raising kids. He argues that some of what money buys the rich, like stability and support, could be offered to families now, through policy aimed at the years when kids are young and budgets are tightest.

Shi’s own fix is more about time than money. She and her husband run BodySpec together, and their favorite workday, she says, is Saturday.

The office is empty, and one of the kids usually tags along, most often her son, the middle child. He gets to build a LEGO set without his sisters taking it apart, then has lunch alone with his mom.

“Nobody told us this counted as work-life balance, but it’s the closest thing we’ve found,” Shi says.

Mammograms May Offer Early Insight Into Cardiovascular Disease

📅 Nov 08, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Mammograms are widely used to help detect breast cancer, but they may also offer insight into heart health.

The routine X-ray screening may identify breast arterial calcification (BAC), which is the presence of calcium deposits within the walls of the small arteries in the breast.

On a mammogram, BAC is commonly identified as thin, white, parallel lines that give a “tram-track” appearance.

While BAC is harmless to the breasts, it is associated with a significantly higher risk of cardiovascular disease. A 2022 study found women with BAC were 51% more likely to develop heart disease or experience a stroke than those without it.

Laxmi Mehta, MD, a cardiologist at The Ohio State University Wexner Medical Center, said BAC is associated with cardiovascular risk factors, such as diabetes, high blood pressure, and high cholesterol. Additionally, BAC has been connected to:

“The reasons for BAC resulting in higher risks for cardiovascular disease are not definitely determined; however, there are some potential mechanisms, one being that they share similar risk factors that result in vascular injury or metabolic stress that result in vascular calcification and atherosclerosis,” Mehta told Healthline.

Cheng-Han Chen, MD, interventional cardiologist and medical director of the Structural Heart Program at MemorialCare Saddleback Medical Center, added that because BAC and heart disease share many of the same risk factors, “calcium in breast arteries therefore suggests disease in the heart (coronary) and brain arteries as well,” he told Healthline.

While 1 in 39 females in the United States dies from breast cancer each year, cardiovascular disease causes one out of every three deaths, resulting in 1 death per minute.

“Cardiovascular disease is still the most common cause of death in premenopausal women,” Richard Reitherman, MD, PhD, a radiologist and medical director of breast imaging at MemorialCare Breast Center at Orange Coast Medical Center, told Healthline.

“The presentation of cardiovascular disease in premenopausal women is notoriously different than in postmenopausal women, primarily [because] there are no typical symptoms such as chest pain.”

Reitherman said that a prevalent and common health screening procedure such as a mammogram can lead to the early identification of women with silent cardiovascular disease.

“Cardiovascular outcome studies that have included breast arterial calcifications as a risk factor are increasingly reported in the medical literature,” he said.

“The hope is that identifying younger women with silent cardiovascular risks [like BAC along with] appropriate integrated risk identification, analysis, and interventions can significantly reduce mortality in this age group.”

Mehta said there are several reasons BAC has been overlooked as a risk factor for heart disease.

“Mammograms are completed for assessment of breast cancer, and BAC has been historically an ‘incidental’ finding, which is not clinically relevant from an oncologic standpoint,” she said.

Additionally, there is no standardization on quantification of BAC severity, nor is it a required component of mammogram reports, said Mehta.

However, routine reporting of BAC on mammograms is an urgent goal of the American College of Radiology, Society of Breast Imaging, and Medicare, said Reitherman.

In fact, it is gaining traction in the United States. A new Maryland law, passed on October 1, requires mammography centers to notify women when a BAC is detected on their mammogram.

“Automated identification and reporting of these calcifications detected on screening mammograms will become increasingly prevalent in the coming months [and] years,” said Reitherman.

The next step is to identify this group of women to primary care physicians so that appropriate risk evaluation can take place, Reitherman added.

In addition to doctors routinely screening for conditions related to heart disease, such as diabetes, high blood pressure, and high cholesterol, he hopes BAC will also be routinely considered.

“Under current development are protocols to identify patients needing a cardiologist’s training and expertise to appropriately stratify this group of patients,” said Reitherman.

“The common healthcare behavior [of] having regular mammograms not only reduces risk of morbidity and mortality from breast cancer but will hopefully contribute to the reduction of morbidity and mortality from cardiovascular disease in younger women.”

He said this type of integrated healthcare approach demonstrates cross-specialty integration and coordination among women, radiologists, and their healthcare providers to improve women’s lives.

“This is an amazing game-changing opportunity,” Reitherman said.

Mehta said it’s reasonable for women to ask if BAC is present on their mammogram report.

“But if it is not uniformly reported by the radiologist, the individual clinician may not have the answer,” she said.

More research is needed before BAC on mammograms can be used routinely to predict heart disease.

Finding out your BAC could provide additional information to help you and your doctors decide how aggressively to address your cardiovascular risk factors, Chen said.

Reitherman agreed. “The goal of reducing morbidity and mortality from any cause such as cardiovascular disease is based on recognition of risk factors, followed by appropriate lifestyle and pharmacologic interventions,” he said.

Why you should never put your PlayStation 5 on a carpeted floor

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Your PS5 needs a better home than a carpet, and you should keep the vents clear, wherever it ends up.

Sony is a bit ambiguous about the PlayStation 5 and carpeted flooring. Whether you read the downloadable PDF guide or the PlayStation Blog, it references avoiding long-fiber carpet only. However, there are three different PS5s in play, which include the Slim, the PS5 Pro and the original model. The original has no vents on its vertical base, while the Slim and the Pro have small vents on the vertical base and come with little rail stands for horizontal placement. With that knowledge, it's reasonable to assume a Slim and Pro can sit horizontally on carpet, and a first-gen PS5 with its base is fine vertically. But with Sony's basic, innocuous statement to go on, there are no distinguishing suggestions for each.

To be clear, you shouldn't place any PS5s on a short-pile or long-pile carpet, because the issue is what's hiding there. Even if you clean it regularly, carpet is a super magnet for every spore, dead skin flake, dust mote, virus and bacteria you can imagine. It's a cornucopia of tiny terror frenzies that, if viewed under a microscope, would have you ripping your carpet out the same day. And you're placing your vent ports within 1 or 2 inches of it.

Besides, putting any console on the floor is practically begging for it to get stepped on or kicked. Long-pile carpets hide things like Lego bricks, gleefully awaiting the approach of bare feet. Step on one of those and you're falling on your PS5 before you know what bit the bottom of your foot. Horizontal is better on a short-pile carpet, but again, those vents are awfully close to an around-the-clock collection of microscopic horrors. If your PS5, regardless of model, has been on carpeted flooring for a while, it's time to make a change and do a PS5 console cleaning to boot.

A small table or an open TV stand is a much better accommodation for a PS5, so long as the console has plenty of room around it. Sony recommends a minimum of 4 inches around each side of the PS5. Jamming it into a cubby hole like a book won't cut it. If that's your only option, you're better off finding a small stand rather than putting it back on the carpet. The vents need to breathe, and the optimal environment is a clean and open one.

The location and placement of a PS5 matters greatly to avoid overheating, so don't worry about whether or not the system is hidden. A Slim model is a little easier to blend in with the surrounding aesthetic, but the Pro and original models are pretty large and tall. If you have to, make the space. That is, if you want your console to last as long as possible. Over time, things change, objects get tossed on the table or someone sets something down and forgets about it. Simple mindfulness will take care of that in short order. Just keep the area clean and maintained. Now and then, give your vents a quick vacuum. Use a low-power handheld and make sure the PS5 is off and unplugged before you start.

Not really. Like any PC that uses fans for cooling, the PS5's fan can get pretty loud at times, depending on what you're playing. That's perfectly normal for the system and isn't a reason to cut the power to the entire house and stuff the console in a freezer. It can overheat if it hasn't been maintained or the placement is terrible. Playing high-performance games for hours on a poorly maintained console in a tight bookshelf will effectively damage its internal components over time. In fact, third-party repair shops like Barrie Screen Repair note that first-gen consoles have had issues with liquid metal thermal paste displacement while in the vertical position for prolonged periods.

Crashing in the middle of a game is an obvious warning sign. Overly loud fans that never drop to a lower speed or "Your PS5 is hot" messages popping up on screen make things pretty clear as well. Fortunately, the system does have fail safes. It will shut down on its own if things get a little too warm, not only to protect internals but to prolong the PS5s lifespan. If these things are happening and your system is sitting on the carpet, take it as a sign. However, once it's off the carpet, it probably needs a routine cleaning and plenty of space to stretch its legs in the future.

If you keep the setup simple and maintain the system regularly, you should be free and clear, barring an entirely separate issue that has nothing to do with carpets or cleaning.

‘Ferritin Face’: What Low Iron Actually Does (and Doesn't Do) to Your Appearance

📅 Nov 07, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Social media influencers claim that having a “ferritin face” may indicate you’re low in iron.

Ferritin is a protein that helps your body store iron. You need iron in order to make healthy red blood cells, which carry oxygen from your lungs to the rest of your body. Iron is also important for bone marrow, organs, healthy muscles, and brain development.

As “ferritin face” makes its way across social media platforms, users are describing several tell-tale signs, including:

Users have also shared images of their face before and after taking iron supplements or getting an iron infusion. Videos about “ferritin face” have garnered millions of views on various platforms.

However, medical professionals state that your appearance alone is not enough to diagnose iron deficiency.

“It’s important to understand that ferritin face is not a medical diagnosis, nor is it a confirmed symptom of iron deficiency. While some people with iron deficiency can appear pale due to low hemoglobin levels in their red blood cells, there are many other symptoms to consider,” said Daniel Atkinson, a general practitioner in the United Kingdom and clinical lead at Treated.

“Notably, these symptoms are also present in many different conditions, and their presence does not necessarily mean iron deficiency anemia or even low ferritin levels. Similar features are seen in people with iron processing and absorption issues, such as gluten intolerance, or in urgent sudden blood loss scenarios,” added Jonathan Jennings, MD, board certified internist at Medical Offices of Manhattan.

Iron deficiency can affect your skin and appearance. These signs are often subtle, but may include:

In some ways, the social media videos are correct. Your skin and face can change when your iron levels are low.

“The facial signs people point to, like pale skin and a washed-out look, mostly come from anaemia, when your haemoglobin has dropped. But iron deficiency and anaemia aren’t the same thing,” said Thom Phillips, a general practitioner in the United Kingdom and Chief Medical Officer at Forth. “By the time low iron shows on your face, your stores are usually close to empty.”

It is important to speak with a healthcare professional, who will run blood tests such as a complete blood count and ferritin levels.

It is also important not to take iron supplements without first checking with your healthcare professional, because having too much iron in your body can also cause negative effects.

Too little and too much iron could raise your risk of infection, which is why it’s important not to take supplements without medical supervision.

The signs and symptoms of iron deficiency can depend on whether you are anemic or not. Some of the early signs may include:

“These symptoms typically occur because your body produces fewer red blood cells, which results in less oxygen being delivered throughout your body, making you feel tired and weak,” said Atkinson.

While your facial appearance can be a sign that something isn’t right, healthcare professionals consider other symptoms and how you feel overall.

They also rely on blood tests to confirm the diagnosis and guide treatment.

“The misinformation is the idea that you can spot it yourself. That leads to two problems: people with low iron who look fine assume they’re OK, and people who look tired start taking iron supplements they don’t need,” said Phillips.

Atkinson also noted that factors such as different settings and environmental conditions can affect how your face appears.

That being said, the “ferritin face” trend may help bring awareness to certain symptoms and when to get them checked.

“[This] trend may raise some awareness about iron deficiency anemia and how low iron levels can impact daily life,” said Atkinson.

“Fatigue and lightheadedness are common side effects of low iron, but it manifests in other ways too, such as paler skin or increased hair shedding, which can be concerning for many people. If you experience these symptoms, you should let your doctor know so they can help treat the condition appropriately,” he said.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/pharma/cms-makes-it-official-codifying-medicare-drug-price-negotiations-rolled-out-under-ira" hreflang="en">CMS makes it official, codifying Medicare drug price negotiations established under IRA</a>

📅 2026-11-06  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
CMS makes it official, codifying Medicare drug price negotiations established under IRA" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Against a backdrop of repeated industry challenges in court, the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services (CMS) is locking in the drug price negotiation framework established under the Biden-era Inflation Reduction Act (IRA).

In turn, the process under which CMS selects certain high-spend Medicare Part B or Part D drugs and negotiates lower prices with manufacturers will carry on into 2029 and beyond. The rule codifies and expands policies previously established through guidance for the initial 2026-28 period.

The final rule is slated to go into effect Dec. 5, CMS said in a fact sheet accompanying Thursday’s announcement. By Feb. 1 of next year, CMS will select up to 20 drugs covered under Medicare Part B or Part D for price negotiations taking effect in 2029, with the potential to also select medicines included in previous rounds for renegotiation.

That upcoming group of drugs will make up the fourth cycle of pricing negotiations, with negotiated prices taking effect in 2029. CMS asserted that the rule gives both patients and drugmakers “a clear path forward as the agency builds on the program’s results and continues negotiating prices for high-cost drugs.”

The initial rounds of price negotiations have already included a slew of prominent brands, including Novo's semaglutide franchise of Wegovy and Ozempic and cancer meds like Bristol Myers Squibb’s Pomalyst, Pfizer’s Ibrance and AstraZeneca’s Calquence.

Meanwhile, the latest tranche of drugs selected earlier this year for negotiation, whose new prices would go into effect in 2028, includes notable entrants like Gilead’s HIV treatment Biktarvy, AbbVie’s Botox and Eli Lilly’s diabetes drug Trulicity, to name just a few.

Beyond codifying the framework for price negotiations in 2029 and subsequent years, CMS has also taken steps to fine-tune several elements of the program.

Namely, the rule finalizes a policy on how CMS classifies certain fixed-combination drugs, with the aim of addressing “potential program integrity concerns” that could encourage drugmakers to evade negotiated prices by steering patients toward new product formulations.

The rule also includes a provision establishing a temporary “small biotech floor” that prevents CMS from offering or accepting a counteroffer below a specified price floor for certain eligible small biotech drugs in 2029 and 2030. It also codifies two policies related to Medicare Part D intended to ensure plan coverage of medicines with a maximum fair price in effect and limit the prices plans pay pharmacies and other dispensing entities.

The IRA and the Medicare price negotiations it empowered have been frequent targets of the biopharma industry, which has broadly alleged that the policy is unconstitutional and that the pricing talks aren’t as voluntary as advertised. But attempts by major drugmakers and powerful trade organizations like the Pharmaceutical Research and Manufacturers of America (PhRMA) to push back on Medicare price negotiations have repeatedly stalled out.

In the interim, the U.S. government has made a separate and more recent push to rein in high prescription drug costs by leaning on President Donald Trump’s “most favored nation” (MFN) strategy.

“President Trump directed us to lower drug costs for Americans using every tool at our disposal,” CMS Administrator Mehmet Oz, M.D., said in a statement announcing the new rule. “This rule provides additional tools to help CMS negotiate better deals, achieve greater savings for taxpayers and beneficiaries, and improve health outcomes for seniors who rely on the Medicare Prescription Drug Program.”

MFN, for its part, has yielded at times, controversial deals with more than two dozen drugmakers, generally involving commitments to price new medicines in line with prices in high-income comparator countries and offer MFN pricing on certain existing drugs through Medicaid.

Through those deals, which have also included investment commitments in the U.S., drugmakers have been able to secure temporary exemptions from the Trump administration’s threatened pharmaceutical import tariffs.

Around this week’s midterm elections, several Big Pharma executives have signaled that they expect little immediate change to their thinking around MFN. Nevertheless, the lack of transparency surrounding the deals has already drawn scrutiny from Democratic lawmakers. With Democrats set to control both chambers of Congress beginning in January, the party may soon press harder to uncover the details of those agreements.

Melissa Joan Hart Explains It All: Hot Flashes, Menopause, and Turning 50

📅 Nov 06, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Actor Melissa Joan Hart has a vivid memory of her mother standing in front of their open freezer to cool off. As a child, she didn’t understand why. Now, as a 50-year-old woman, Hart knows that her mom was experiencing hot flashes.

“[She’s] only 20 years older than me, so maybe I missed the part where she was going through [menopause], but there was no conversation about it,” Hart told Healthline. “I feel like it’s just becoming a hot topic with women, where…the taboo is going away from it now.”

Her own perimenopausal symptoms started with night sweats.

“[It] was really jarring, and the first few times, you’re not really sure what that was…do I need to turn the air down or what’s going on here?” Hart said.

Over time, she learned that she was experiencing moderate to severe hot flashes, which affect up to 80% of women during the menopause transition and can persist for 7 to 10 years.

Doctors categorize hot flashes as mild, moderate, or severe, said Diana Bitner, MD, OB-GYN, at Corewell Health.

“[So] if they’re mild, it’s a sense of warmth (maybe in the chest, or the neck, or the face). A moderate hot flash is where there’s associated sweating, and a severe hot flash is something that stops you in your tracks, wakes you up from the middle of the night,” Bitner told Healthline.

Hart has mostly experienced hot flashes while home or at her three sons’ activities, and they have yet to interfere with her work. However, according to research, one in three women report moderate to severe challenges in managing their work due to symptoms related to menopause.

“It’s just so sad that about 10% of women leave the workforce due to menopause,” said Bitner. “And so it can really affect our ability to perform at these key years of not only institutional memory, but also of earning power, and it can really affect the whole community.”

Bitner said women should feel empowered to bring up their menopausal symptoms or questions with their doctor.

“[This] is something that was taboo for so long…women didn’t really talk about this, whether it was because of embarrassment, or [they] weren’t sure if anyone was experiencing it,” she said. “[There’s] no reason, in this day and age, we need to suffer through it…it’s something that there are treatment options for.”

In fact, Bitner said providing specific details about their symptoms, such as how many mild, moderate, or severe hot flashes they’ve experienced over the past week, can help doctors better assess patients’ needs.

Hart plans to discuss treatment options for her hot flashes with her doctor soon. In the last few years, she said she has put emphasis on her midlife health.

“I just was sick of waking up with aches and pains in general [thinking], ‘is this the rest of my life?’” she said.

She started listening to self-help books in the car while driving her kids around and found ways to put herself first.

“My kids are a little bit older, so I kind of have that freedom to be able to do that,” she said. “[But] along with getting older, comes the menopause, comes that different time in life. And I think it’s a good time to reevaluate [how] our bodies can’t handle what [they] used to handle, and let’s figure it out.”

Hart has partnered with pharmaceutical company Bayer on its “Life Doesn’t Stop for a Hot Flash” campaign, which encourages women to discuss menopause symptoms with their doctors and promotes Lynkuet, a hormone-free prescription medication for moderate to severe hot flashes associated with menopause.

“It’s called ‘Life Doesn’t Stop for a Hot Flash’ campaign because it shouldn’t, because we should be able to push through, and this should be a time in our lives when our kids are getting older, and we’re able to kind of reinvent ourselves,” said Hart. “And I think it’s an exciting time, and it shouldn’t be something that we’re afraid of.”

She is embracing speaking openly about midlife health.

“I hit 50 this year, and it was, like, a huge milestone, and I think that’s something to be proud of, and not something to shy away from,” she said.

Hart also hopes to challenge the longstanding barriers women face in Hollywood as they age, including fewer opportunities for leading roles.

“[I] think we’re seeing that the generation before, and my generation, it feels like we’re pushing those boundaries…and we’re saying, you know, that women at this age have a lot to give, have a lot of information to share, have a lot of stories to tell and experiences and adventures still ahead of us, and, you know, we’re not ready to stop,” Hart said.

In everyday life, Bitner said that while some patients avoid talking about midlife issues like menopause with their doctors, she is seeing more patients who are confident being forthcoming.

As more women talk openly about menopause, she hopes to include men in the conversation, too. In fact, she invites her patients to bring their partners to appointments, so they can gain an understanding of menopause and the experiences their partners face, such as hot flashes, sleep issues, vaginal dryness, and discomfort or pain during intercourse.

“[Often], there’s, you know, comments that come up [from men who] say, ‘How do I talk about this without getting in trouble?’” said Bitner. “[We] want everyone to join this conversation…it doesn’t have to be just her issue because we know it’s not.”

New Food Pyramid Puts Healthy Carbs and Plant-Based Foods First

📅 Nov 05, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

A first-of-its-kind international food pyramid, developed by 22 leading nutritionists from Europe, Canada, the United States, and Australia, was published in Advances in Nutrition in September.

This new food pyramid emphasizes plant-based protein sources, cooking with plant-based oils, and healthier sugars and carbohydrates.

It also echoes the traditional food pyramid used in the United States since 1992.

The new pyramid breaks away from the Trump administration’s updated nutritional guidelines, which are part of Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr.’s Make America Health Again (MAHA) agenda and prioritize red meat over plants.

The new pyramid was designed by a research team led by Brigham Young University professor Karen Della Corte. The researchers noted that this is the first international food pyramid that is designed to reflect scientific agreement across multiple countries and cultures.

Della Corte and researchers from the International Carbohydrate Quality Consortium (ICQC), compared dietary guidelines from multiple countries, including:

They also analyzed evidence from more than 250 scientific publications, while drawing on the EAT-Lancet Planetary Health Diet to identify principles of healthy eating that may be shared.

The new pyramid highlights three guidelines:

While the pyramid emphasizes plant-based proteins, it doesn’t exclude animal proteins.

“I find the new international food pyramid a more practical and aligned approach for the prevention of chronic disease,” said Michelle Routhenstein, Preventive Cardiology Dietitian at Entirely Nourished, who wasn’t involved in the creation of the food pyramid.

“The new international food pyramid places greater emphasis on food quality and the importance of building meals around nutrient-dense plant foods, while still allowing room for lean animal proteins such as fish, poultry, eggs, and yogurt,” she added.

While the new pyramid and the current U.S. dietary guidelines differ in many ways, they also agree on certain factors.

For example, both sets of guidelines discourage added sugars, refined carbs, and highly processed snacks.

The differences between the two mainly lie in their emphasis on specific sources of protein and fat. For example, the new pyramid prioritizes plant-based proteins, while the U.S. guidelines place greater emphasis on animal proteins, including red meat.

While both sets of recommendations advise limiting saturated fat, the new pyramid emphasizes unsaturated plant oils, whereas the U.S. guidelines also include butter and beef tallow as cooking fat options.

Routhenstein noted that an important distinction is that this new pyramid goes beyond simply telling people to eat more protein or fewer carbohydrates. Instead, it encourages people to consider the quality and sources of those nutrients.

However, the main emphasis of both guidelines is encouraging people to eat fewer ultra-processed foods and more natural or “real” foods.

Routhenstein told Healthline that research consistently shows that plant-forward eating patterns, including the Mediterranean and DASH diets, are associated with better cardiovascular health and a lower risk of heart disease.

Plant foods provide fiber, potassium, antioxidants, and other beneficial compounds that can help support healthy cholesterol levels, blood pressure, blood sugar, and blood vessel function.

“This is not to say that protein and fat are unimportant, but their sources matter,” Routhenstein said.

In a statement from BYU, Della Corte shared the following tips for incorporating the new pyramid’s recommendations into your diet:

The pyramid is a framework, not a personalized prescription. If you have questions about your individual nutritional needs, you can speak with your healthcare professional or a licensed dietitian.

“It is important to recognize that plant-forward does not mean vegan or vegetarian. The amount and sources of animal protein should be considered to ensure they align with an individual’s cardiometabolic goals and lab targets,” Routhenstein said.

An Expert Guide to Nail Health, According to Professionals

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Here’s exactly how to keep them strong and healthy

Every product on this page was chosen by a Harper's BAZAAR editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

As manicure trends evolve with new lacquers, changing shapes, and viral techniques, beauty lovers often compromise nail health for aesthetics. While many nail formulas have improved over the years to prioritize strength, uniformity, and finish, experts say incremental habits—from lifestyle shifts to resisting the urge to peel off chipped polish—can ultimately play the biggest role in keeping your nails in excellent condition.

“The future of manicures isn’t just about the latest color trends or how long your gel polish lasts, but how well we care for the nails underneath,” Krisztina van der Boom, founder of DryBy, previously told Harper’s Bazaar. Hand, foot, and nail expert Margaret Dabbs agrees, urging nail enthusiasts to “Adopt a dedicated nail care routine and stick to it.” But what does an effective routine actually look like? Along with keeping your nails clean and hydrated with an emollient moisturizer, like Caudalíe’s Vinotherapist Hand and Nail Cream, professionals suggest keeping a cuticle oil, such as Dior’s Huile Abricot, in your bag for quick touch-ups.

“Cuticle oil is the one product I would never skip,” says Peach Bling founder Sandra Wei. “Look for a formula with jojoba oil, which has small enough molecules to absorb into the nail and surrounding skin.” Wei encourages her clients to apply it daily, ideally before bed, and to treat it as a small ritual. “A rich hand cream and gloves for dishwashing and cleaning also make a real difference,” continues Wei, “since water and harsh detergents are among the most common causes of dry, weak nails.” Maintaining nail and cuticle hydration is also key for mature nails, as they tend to grow more slowly and hold less moisture as we age, which can contribute to brittleness and vertical ridges.

Professionals have debated whether it’s safe to trim your cuticles, since cuticles guard nail beds against bacteria and infection; that's why they often advise pushing them back instead of removing them entirely. “As long as you're not cutting any live skin and damaging your cuticles, gently pushing them back and buffing away excess skin will actually help improve the health and the appearance of your cuticles and will help make your manicure last as long as possible while looking salon-quality,” says Olivia Van Iderstine, vice president of education and manicure trend expert at Olive & June. Prep your fingertips with cuticle oil or a soak in warm, soapy water before a manicure, then use a disposable orangewood stick or stainless steel pusher, such as JinSoon’s HyperCare Cuticle Pusher + Reducer, to improve polish cohesion and create an elongated nail look.

Consistent, gentle filing can also keep nails strong, regardless of your shape preference, because a smooth surface guards against unwanted splits, rips, and tears that can cause long-term damage. While there’s nothing wrong with a budget-friendly emery board, professionals say investing in the right tools provides the right resistance without compromising your nails' integrity. Dabbs is a fan of crystal nail files because they can help prevent micro-splits that lead to peeling and breakage, though the best results depend on not overfiling.

And if you usually opt for gel polish manicures, they don’t have to be a deal breaker for nail health. Several options, from builder gel to Japanese gel and more, provide longevity, durability, and style without weakening natural nails.

“Japanese gel systems are known for their precision and for their focus on preserving the natural nail,” continues Wei. “The prep is minimal and gentle, so we avoid heavy buffing of the nail plate. The gel itself is applied in fine, structured layers that are flexible and sit close to the natural nail.” Peach Bling prioritizes formulas that are free of HEMA and TPO, two ingredients commonly linked to gel allergies and sensitivities. “The result is a manicure that looks refined, wears beautifully, and treats the nail underneath with care,” explains the founder. Bazaar editors love builder gel, as it works like Japanese gel, supporting natural nails with a rounded apex to reinforce their sturdiness.

Experts advise keeping a gel set on for no longer than four weeks, as regrowth changes the nail's balance, ultimately causing unwanted stress and making breaks more likely. Once gel polish begins to lift or crack, it’s often a sign that removal is necessary—and that process can often be more damaging than simply wearing a gel formula in the first place. “What protects nail integrity is how the gel is applied and, even more importantly, how it is removed,” adds Wei. “With careful, gentle removal, there is no need to take a ‘break’ between appointments.”

Every story we publish has been thoroughly researched and vetted by our team of beauty editors and industry experts.

What Is Rhabdo? What Every Woman Should Know About the Rare Condition

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

A woman’s death during the New York City Marathon has put the condition back in the spotlight.

At the New York City Marathon on Sunday, 24-year-old Rachel Apa of Lewisberry, Pennsylvania, collapsed two miles from the finish line. She later died at the hospital.

The medical examiner’s office is still investigating her cause of death. Her father, Steve Apa, told the New York Times that the cause was rhabdomyolysis, commonly known as rhabdo. The rare condition occurs when skeletal muscle breaks down, releasing its contents into the bloodstream, says Robert Glatter, MD, an emergency room doctor and an assistant professor of emergency medicine at Lenox Hill Hospital.

Apa’s death comes amid growing conversations about rhabdo on social media. People have shared their experiences with the condition after intense exercise, particularly at cycling classes. https://www.tiktok.com/@bryybell_/video/7693630991839153421?_r=1&_t=ZT-9Axae4rjSXu

But while these stories are alarming, rhabdo is still relatively rare. Yet they raise questions about the risks of intense exercise, how to recognize when something is wrong, and just how concerned the everyday exerciser should be. Here’s what experts want you to know.

Rhabdomyolysis happens when skeletal muscle breaks down and releases its contents into the bloodstream. The resulting spill can produce dangerous electrolyte imbalances and kidney injury, particularly when someone is dehydrated, Dr. Glatter says.

Although rhabdo is often associated with intense exercise, physical exertion is just one possible cause. “Rhabdomyolysis can also follow crush injuries, seizures, prolonged immobilization, heat stroke, severe infections, certain drugs or toxins, metabolic abnormalities and, more rarely, inherited muscle disorders,” Dr. Glatter says.

Exercise-related rhabdo typically happens when an individual suddenly performs an unusually difficult, repetitive workout involving large muscle groups—particularly when doing eccentric exercise, or when the muscle contracts while lengthening. The risk may be higher when the activity is unfamiliar or far more intense than the person is accustomed to. Some examples include high-repetition squats, lunges, downhill running, and intense cycling, Dr. Glatter says.

“The key issue is often not simply that the workout was ‘hard,’ but that its intensity or volume was far beyond what that person’s muscles were conditioned to tolerate,” he says.

Symptoms include muscle pain "dramatically out of proportion to ordinary post-workout ache,” Dr. Glatter describes, as well as dark-colored urine, swelling, weakness, and difficulty moving the affected muscles. Other possible symptoms include dizziness, fainting, confusion, persistent vomiting or feeling generally ill, he says. “Someone who develops severe or rapidly worsening muscle pain or swelling after a strenuous workout—especially together with marked weakness, dark urine or substantially decreased urination—should be evaluated urgently.”

It’s important to note that symptoms can start while someone is still exercising. An athlete may notice muscle pain that feels disproportionate to effort, unusual heaviness or weakness, severe fatigue, or a drop in pace or intensity that would normally be manageable, Dr. Glatter says. In severe cases, athletes can collapse, appear confused, or develop nausea, dizziness, and swelling.

Other risk factors also include dehydration, exercising in extreme heat or humidity, a recent viral illness, certain medications or drugs, and underlying genetic or metabolic disorders, such as sickle cell.

Although many recent social media accounts of exercise-related rhabdo have come from women, it’s important to know that an unaccustomed high-intensity workout can overwhelm muscles regardless of sex. Those stories don’t necessarily reflect who is most likely to develop the condition, and studies have shown generally higher rates in men.

It’s complicated. “It’s probably a combination of greater awareness and a genuine increase in some types of exertional rhabdomyolysis, but the evidence does not support describing rhabdo as a new epidemic,” Dr. Glatter says.

Rhabdo has been around for decades, and it’s difficult to determine overall incidence rates because mild cases may never be diagnosed. Still, some research suggests that exercise-related cases have increased in certain settings. A 2022 analysis published in the Physician and Sportsmedicine looked at emergency department data from 2000 through 2019. It estimated more than 40,000 cases of exertional rhabdomyolysis and found an increase over that period.

Dr. Glatter suggests this might be, in part, due to shifts in exercise culture. An increase in the availability of fitness classes such as high-intensity interval training, cycling, and CrossFit-style workouts can expose people to unfamiliar movement patterns at high intensities.

“These workouts are not inherently dangerous, and millions of people perform them safely. The risk arises when workload increases too quickly relative to conditioning, particularly when heat, dehydration, illness, medications or inadequate recovery are added to the equation,” Dr. Glatter says. “A highly motivated newcomer trying to keep pace with a class may continue well past the point at which her muscles are accustomed to working.”

It’s also important to note: Being well-trained does lower the risk of rhabdo, but it does not eliminate it. “In a trained athlete, rhabdomyolysis is often best understood as a ‘multiple-hit’ phenomenon, where several physiologic stresses occur at the same time,” Dr. Glatter says. Someone may be proportionally prepared for the activity, but factors such as unusually high intensity or duration, heat and humidity, dehydration, recent illness, inadequate recovery, electrolyte abnormalities, and certain medications or supplements can lower the threshold for injury. Rare underlying conditions, such as sickle cell trait or certain metabolic conditions, can also predispose a healthy athlete to rhabdo.

That doesn’t mean you should fear strenuous exercise. Starting at a manageable level, building up gradually, and staying hydrated can help reduce the risk.

“The body adapts extraordinarily well to exercise when intensity and volume are increased gradually,” Dr. Glatter says. “Going from relatively little training to an all-out, high-repetition workout involving large muscle groups is a very different physiological challenge.”

Exercise-related rhabdo is uncommon, but can happen when a workout places demands on muscles that are far beyond what they’re accustomed to. High-intensity exercise can be part of a healthy routine; the key is to build toward it gradually and pay attention to how your body responds. If you develop severe muscle pain or swelling, weakness, dark urine, or substantially decreased urination, seek urgent medical care.

Cori Ritchey, C.S.C.S., is the fitness editor at Women's Health, as well as a certified strength and condition coach and group fitness instructor. She’s reported on topics regarding health, nutrition, mental health, fitness, sex, and relationships for several years. You can find more of her work in Men’s Health, HealthCentral, Livestrong, Self, and others.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/providers/commonspirit-health-talks-hand-3-more-north-dakota-hospitals-altru-health-system" hreflang="en">CommonSpirit Health signs definitive agreement to sell 3 more N.D. hospitals to Altru Health System</a>

📅 2026-11-04  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
CommonSpirit Health signs definitive agreement to sell 3 more N.D. hospitals to Altru Health System" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

CommonSpirit Health has locked in a deal to sell three hospitals to Altru Health System, rural North Dakota nonprofit.

The deal is contingent on regulatory review and other customary closing conditions, but expected to close in early 2027. The pair's new definitive agreement, announced Wednesday, follows 10 months of due diligence following a letter of intent unveiled near the top of the year (see that story below). Financial details were not disclosed.

The to-be-sold facilities are CHI St. Alexius Health Bismarck, a multispecialty acute care medical center in the state’s capital, and two smaller critical access hospitals, CHI St. Alexius Health Turtle Lake and CHI St. Alexius Health Garrison.

They would join Altru's flagship acute care hospital, a critical access hospital it also purchased from CommonSpirit in March, a trauma center, a specialty hospital and 20 practices spread across the northern part of the state.

The organizations said there are no changes to patient care or day-to-day operations related to the deal. The hospitals will maintain their Catholic identity and mission.

"Through our work together over the years, we’ve developed tremendous confidence in Altru’s commitment to high-quality, compassionate care," Tim Bricker, president of CommonSpirit Health's Central Region, said in reference to prior partnerships with the  We believe Altru is the right partner to build on St. Alexius’ proud legacy, honor its Catholic mission and continue serving patients and families for generations to come.”

CommonSpirit is in the midst of a sweeping operational overhaul as it looks to bolster its financial performance ahead of imminent Medicaid cuts. Those efforts have opened the $42.4 billion system to a string of hospital divestitures, including its four handoffs to Altru as well as the sale of a four-hospital Ohio system to UPMC in September.

CommonSpirit Health and Altru Health System are considering a deal to transfer three North Dakota hospitals to the latter, the organizations announced Thursday.

The pair’s signed letter of intent outlines plans to evaluate a potential deal for the facilities, a process they said would run “the next several months” before a potential definitive agreement might be struck.

In the balance are CHI St. Alexius Health Bismarck, a multispecialty acute care medical center in the state’s capital, and two smaller critical access hospitals, CHI St. Alexius Health Turtle Lake and CHI St. Alexius Health Garrison. All three are Catholic facilities within CommonSpirit’s regional healthcare system CHI St. Alexius Health.

Potential financial terms of any deal for the hospitals were not disclosed, nor were any details on whether other assets tied to the hospitals, such as clinics, would be included.

“This is a natural next step in exploring how we can continue to support strong, sustainable healthcare for the region,” Tim Bricker, president of CommonSpirit Health’s central region, which includes CHI St. Alexius Health, said in the announcement.

CommonSpirit is a $40 billion nonprofit that counted 137 hospitals in two dozen states. It’s in the midst of an operating performance turnaround initiative and, at this month’s J.P. Morgan Healthcare Conference, teased “a couple” of portfolio divestitures to be announced in the next quarter as part of the effort.

Grand Forks, North Dakota-based Altru Health System is a smaller nonprofit, with just over $700 million of revenue in 2024 from its sole acute care hospital, a specialty hospital and 20 practice locations in northeast North Dakota and northwest Minnesota. It opened a new $500 million hospital in January 2025.

The news comes hot on the heels of another deal between the systems for ownership of CHI St. Alexius Health Devils Lake, another critical access hospital. That handoff was unveiled in December and is set to close March 1.

Both deals have been described as a boon for rural communities and an opportunity for Altru to better coordinate its local care.

“Altru has a long history of serving communities across North Dakota and northwest Minnesota, and our organizations share a deep respect for the people we serve and the responsibility that comes with delivering local healthcare,” Todd Forkel, CEO of Altru, said in the announcement. “We look forward to working more closely with CommonSpirit to strengthen access to care for patients and families well into the future.”

Energy Drinks, Stimulant Supplements Linked to Higher Heart Risks in Young Adults

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Your hub for heart health management, insights, and solutions

Learn the basics to keeping your heart healthy

New research suggests that caffeinated energy drinks and stimulant supplements are associated with significantly higher risks of serious heart problems, including cardiac arrest and heart attack in young adults.

The findings, to be presented at the American Heart Association (AHA) Scientific Sessions 2026 November 6–9 in Chicago, are preliminary and can’t prove that energy drinks cause heart problems.

Still, the results highlight concerning cardiovascular effects of these popular products, particularly among adults under 35 who consume them regularly.

“These products are marketed as everyday pick-me-ups and sold next to soda and bottled water, so most people assume they carry no real risk,” study author Sai Samayamanthula, a fourth-year medical student at the University of Virginia School of Medicine in Charlottesville, VA, said in a news release.

“For many healthy people, if these products are consumed occasionally, the risk is probably small. However, individuals who consume energy drinks or other stimulant-containing supplements regularly can quickly exceed the recommended daily limit for caffeine, so it’s important for people to know that there may be a risk of cardiovascular harm with these products,” Samayamanthula continued.

Researchers from the University of Virginia School of Medicine analyzed 28,345 reports submitted to the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) between 2017 and 2025.

Of these, 854 involved caffeinated energy drinks or stimulant supplements, while the remaining reports involved non-stimulant products.

Compared with non-stimulant products, cardiac arrest was reported approximately 11 times more often with energy drinks and stimulant supplements. Heart attacks were reported 4.5 times more often, while ventricular arrhythmias and palpitations were reported about four times more frequently.

Nearly 45% of heart-related reports involved adults ages 18 to 34.

“Our findings support the case for more systematic safety monitoring of caffeine- and stimulant-containing products. In addition, healthcare professionals should remember to ask their younger patients who have palpitations, chest pain or fainting spells if they recently or regularly consume these products,” Samayamanthula said.

Gregory M. Marcus, MD, professor of medicine at the University of California, San Francisco School of Medicine, and chair of the 2026 AHA scientific statement on caffeine and cardiovascular disease, wasn’t involved in the study, but said the research “raises concerns that products with very high amounts of caffeine may be harmful to the cardiovascular system.”

“Although there are biological reasons why that connection could be possible, the study does not prove that caffeine products cause these events, because other factors may have influenced the results found in this analysis,” Marcus said in a news release.

“More research is needed to understand whether these stimulant products are truly responsible for the reported heart-related events.”

Cheng-Han Chen, MD, board certified interventional cardiologist and medical director of the Structural Heart Program at MemorialCare Saddleback Medical Center in Laguna Hills, CA, said the findings, although concerning, are not surprising. Chen wasn’t involved in the study.

“We know that energy drinks can have significant effects on the cardiovascular system, primarily because of their stimulant properties,” he told Healthline.

“Caffeine can increase blood pressure and heart rate, while other ingredients, such as guarana and taurine, may further contribute to cardiovascular stimulation and increased stress hormone levels. What is particularly concerning is the potential for these products to trigger serious complications, including abnormal heart rhythms, especially in people with underlying cardiovascular conditions.”

Chen agreed that occasional energy drink consumption is unlikely to have long-term consequences for heart health in most healthy individuals. However, he said that “frequent or excessive consumption can potentially increase the risk of hypertension and cardiac arrhythmias.”

An AHA scientific statement published in July 2026 notes that up to 400 milligrams of caffeine daily is generally considered safe for most healthy adults. That’s roughly equivalent to four or five 8-ounce cups of brewed coffee, depending on how it’s prepared.

However, some energy drinks contain up to three times as much caffeine per fluid ounce as brewed coffee.

Certain energy drinks may also contain stimulants, such as guarana, yerba mate, theobromine, synephrine, and yohimbine.

These ingredients may compound caffeine’s effects on the cardiovascular system.

“Caffeine stimulates the cardiovascular system, primarily by increasing sympathetic nervous system activity. This can cause the heart to beat faster, raise blood pressure, and increase the workload on the heart,” Chen said.

“Energy drinks may contain additional stimulants, such as guarana and taurine, which can further increase blood pressure and stress hormone levels. With excessive consumption, these effects can become more pronounced and potentially interfere with the heart’s normal electrical activity, increasing the risk of abnormal heart rhythms. People with underlying cardiovascular conditions, particularly hypertension or a predisposition to arrhythmias, may be especially vulnerable to these effects.”

Other research has echoed these concerns. A 2025 review suggests that high-volume energy drink consumption can prolong the heart’s QTc interval, increase blood pressure, and raise heart rate beyond the effects of caffeine alone.

“There is growing concern about the cardiovascular effects of energy drinks and stimulant-containing supplements, particularly among younger adults,” Chen said.

“One challenge is that younger individuals may not recognize that these products can have significant effects on their heart and blood pressure, especially when consumed frequently or in combination with other caffeinated products.”

Young adults accounted for a substantial portion of the FDA reports included in the study, highlighting potential cardiovascular concerns for this age group.

People with existing heart conditions, a history of irregular heart rhythms, or heightened caffeine sensitivity may face additional risks. Those with undiagnosed genetic heart conditions may also be vulnerable.

A 2024 study identified cases of sudden cardiac arrest occurring shortly after energy drink consumption, particularly among people with underlying inherited heart conditions.

“As a cardiologist, I am particularly concerned about people who may have underlying cardiovascular conditions that have not yet been diagnosed,” Chen said.

“Excessive stimulant intake can potentially trigger symptoms such as palpitations, chest discomfort, and lightheadedness. In susceptible individuals, it may also increase the risk of more serious complications, including abnormal heart rhythms.”

Additionally, combining energy drinks with strenuous exercise or alcohol may introduce additional cardiovascular risks.

The findings suggest an association between energy drinks, stimulant supplements, and reported cardiovascular events, but they are preliminary and do not establish cause and effect.

The FDA database also relied on voluntary adverse-event reports, meaning some events may never be reported, while others may receive disproportionate attention.

Researchers also do not yet know how many people consumed these products without experiencing any problems. Without this variable, the reports cannot establish actual risk.

Other factors, including underlying health conditions, medications, and lifestyle habits, may also influence the results. More systematic research and controlled clinical trials are needed.

For most healthy adults, consuming up to 400 milligrams of caffeine per day is generally considered safe.

However, Chen noted that individual tolerance for caffeine may vary, and people with underlying cardiovascular conditions, particularly high blood pressure or abnormal heart rhythms, may need to limit or avoid caffeine altogether.

“Energy drinks can vary considerably in their caffeine content and serving sizes,” Chen said.

“Depending on the product, a single can may contain anywhere from 80 to 300 milligrams of caffeine. This means that consuming even one or two energy drinks can put someone close to or above the recommended daily limit, particularly when combined with coffee, tea, or other caffeinated supplements.”

Seek emergency medical attention if you experience chest pain, particularly if it’s accompanied by shortness of breath, fainting, or other symptoms of a possible heart attack.

If you live with a heart condition, ask your doctor about your daily caffeine intake to determine whether you need to cut back.

According to Chen, warning signs of excessive stimulant intake may include:

“Depending on the severity of these symptoms, medical attention may be necessary,” he said.

Still, most people may not need to give up caffeine entirely. Monitoring your habits could help protect your heart health. This may include:

“I encourage people to read product labels carefully and be aware of their total daily caffeine consumption,” Chen said. “I particularly recommend that people with underlying cardiovascular conditions avoid energy drinks.”
