Healthier Salt Substitute May Prevent Millions of Deaths From Heart Disease

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

A simple swap for salt could spare millions of cardiovascular disease-related deaths, new research suggests.

In a study published in Nature Health on November 4, researchers at The George Institute for Global Health modeled the potential impact of 193 countries switching from regular table salt to potassium-enriched salt.

They found the switch could prevent nearly 3 million deaths and more than 10 million new cases of heart and kidney disease worldwide each year.

In the United States, this simple salt swap could prevent nearly 105,000 deaths and about 492,000 new cases a year, the researchers estimated.

About 130.6 million U.S. adults have cardiovascular disease, according to the American Heart Association (AHA).

Liping Huang, PhD, a senior data analyst in the Food Policy Division at The George Institute for Global Health and co-lead author of the study, told Healthline the team started with current blood pressure levels by sex, age, and country.

The researchers then estimated how replacing all salt with a salt substitute would change people’s sodium and potassium intake, blood pressure, and disease risk, she said.The team modeled a blend of 75% sodium chloride and 25% potassium chloride. The study estimated these yearly results worldwide:

The authors wrote that the model relied on data from a few years ago and on many assumptions, and that any large-scale switch would take years.

Potassium-enriched salt replaces some of the sodium chloride in regular table salt with potassium chloride, according to The George Institute.Asked where other salts like kosher salt and sea salt fit in, Huang said those salts may contain some trace elements “which has those health halos around them, but essentially they’re still primarily sodium chloride.”Huang noted that potassium-enriched salt tastes “about the same as a regular table salt.” She said it’s possible a small number of people with a sensitive palate could taste a difference.

Huang said that “it’s really hard for people to cut back their salt intake.”“But the easier way is just to replace part of the sodium chloride in our normal salt with potassium chloride, because increasing potassium intake also helps to reduce blood pressure,” she said.The two minerals play opposite roles in the body, according to Cheng-Han Chen, MD, a board certified interventional cardiologist and medical director of the Structural Heart Program at MemorialCare Saddleback Medical Center in Laguna Hills, CA. Chen wasn’t involved in the study.

“While sodium causes water retention and increased blood pressure, potassium will help the kidneys remove sodium from the body and lower blood pressure,” Chen told Healthline.Huang said she worked on an earlier trial of about 21,000 people in 600 rural villages in northern China in 2021.

People who used potassium-enriched salt had a 14% lower risk of stroke, a 13% lower risk of cardiovascular events, and a 12% lower risk of early death than those who used regular salt, according to a 2021 study in The New England Journal of Medicine that summarized the trial’s results.

The World Health Organization (WHO) endorsed potassium-enriched salt in a 2025 guideline, saying it can reduce blood pressure and the risk of cardiovascular disease.

The AHA said potassium-based salt substitutes can help lower blood pressure in some adults. The Food and Drug Administration (FDA) issued labeling guidance in 2020 allowing an alternate name for potassium chloride on ingredient labels to include the word “salt.”“We think that blood pressure begins to improve as soon as 1 week after cutting back on sodium intake. The full benefit typically takes up to 4 weeks,” Chen said.

“We generally recommend a daily sodium limit of 2,300 milligrams (mg), ideally under 1,500 mg a day. Most people consume over 3,000 mg of sodium a day, well above the recommended limit,” Chen said.The WHO recommends less than 2,000 mg of sodium per day and suggests at least 3,510 mg of potassium a day.In the United States, more than 70% of salt intake comes from processed and restaurant foods, according to the AHA.

Huang said that some people with advanced chronic kidney disease should avoid potassium-enriched salt.When the kidneys don’t work well, the body may not clear extra potassium efficiently, she said. That can lead to hyperkalemia, or high potassium levels in the blood, which can raise the risk of cardiac arrest, Huang said.Among people with stage 3 to stage 5 kidney disease, switching to salt substitutes could prevent about 750,000 deaths a year by lowering blood pressure and cause about 100,000 deaths a year from hyperkalemia, the researchers estimated.The AHA said that people with kidney disease or who take medications that affect potassium should check with a healthcare professional before using these products.

An estimated 37 million U.S. adults have chronic kidney disease, according to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC).

Potassium-enriched salt is available at supermarkets in the U.S. Many major U.S. grocery chains, including Kroger, Walmart, and Publix, carry two types of products, according to their websites:

Potassium-enriched salt typically costs about twice as much as regular salt, according to the study.Huang said she hoped people would see the results and start thinking of the switch as “a simple thing I can do for their own health.”

Democrat Abdul El-Sayed defeats Republican Mike Rogers in key Michigan Senate race

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Abdul El-Sayed speaks to supporters at an Oct. 24 rally in Detroit. Spencer Platt/Getty Images hide caption

Midterms coverage: Live results | What to know | Stories around the countryStay connected: NPR Politics Podcast | NPR Politics newsletter | NPR app

DETROIT — Democrat Abdul El-Sayed has won the U.S. Senate election in Michigan, according to a race call by the Associated Press.

One of the most closely watched elections in the U.S., the race in Michigan tested how an antiestablishment candidate on the left would fare in a perennial Midwest battleground.

El-Sayed will be the first Muslim American to serve in the Senate.

The 42-year-old former public health official defeated former Republican Rep. Mike Rogers. Rogers lost his first bid for the Senate in 2024.

Harnessing anger over the cost of living and corruption, El-Sayed galvanized young and infrequent voters by focusing on passing single-payer healthcare and curbing the influence of corporate money in politics.

He is not a democratic socialist, unlike several candidates whose insurgent campaigns gained traction in primaries this cycle, though his campaign was backed by some of the biggest stars on the political left.

His positions on Israel alienated some Jewish voters, including comments after a truck-ramming attack at a synagogue preschool in metro Detroit — for which he later offered an apology — and campaigning during the primary with controversial left-wing streamer Hasan Piker.

Those were among the dynamics during a heated primary contest that turned into a stand-in for many of the broader debates fueling the direction of the Democratic Party after two years in the political wilderness.

In the primary, El-Sayed narrowly defeated Rep. Haley Stevens by about 1 percentage point, despite Michigan heavyweights like Gov. Gretchen Whitmer and retiring Sen. Gary Peters backing his opponent.

Mike Rogers speaks to supporters during a campaign event at Hoffman Farms Winery on Oct. 9 in Highland Township, Mich. Chip Somodevilla/Getty Images hide caption

In the general election's closing days, Stevens reversed course and announced she was endorsing Rogers, after initially saying she would support the Democratic ticket, underscoring the rocky path to uniting the Democratic coalition in Michigan. After the primary, polls showed a tight race with Rogers, despite Democrats having held the Senate seat since 1979.

During the campaign, El-Sayed criticized his opponent and other Republicans, including President Trump, for targeting him with Islamophobic rhetoric.

Trump early on endorsed Rogers, who has embraced some of the president's false claims about widespread election fraud, despite previously condemning them, including after the Jan. 6, 2021, attack on the U.S. Capitol. As November neared, Rogers tried to distance himself from some of the president's policies, including the war in Iran.

El-Sayed, a Michigan native who ran the health departments in Wayne County and the city of Detroit, first arrived on the political stage in 2018 with an unsuccessful bid for governor.

Now as Democrats wrestle with their party's identity, El-Sayed has signaled that he intends to be a prominent voice, especially given Michigan's prime position in the presidential nominating process in 2028.

Bashpole Location Guide For Phantom Blade Zero

📅 Nov 04, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

In Phantom Blade Zero, Phantom Edges are among the most useful tools in Soul's repertoire. While they have their uses as weapons, some of them, like the Bashpole, are great for getting around and uncovering hidden secrets throughout the world.

Even though the Bashpole is required to access some spots in the game's early locations, you can't get it until much, much later. Luckily, being able to fast travel around the map makes going back to these spots easier, so it's easy to wonder just how to get this Phantom Edge. Here's where to get the Bashpole in Phantom Blade Zero.

To get the Bashpole, you'll need to get into the Mandate Encampment. This comes later than it might initially seem, since when Soul arrives in the Village of Lu, he mentions that the road splits: one way leads to the village, and the other leads to the Mandate Encampment.

This makes it seem like you can go to the Mandate Encampment first, but this isn't the case. Instead, you have to get through the whole Village of Lu, then go through Pangzhen until you beat Gu Wanyue, The Wailing Wraith. After you beat her, you can enter the Well of Wei Sanchuan, where you'll find the key to the Mandate Encampment.

Travel to the gate, go through and make your way through the Mandate Encampment until you reach the bell titled Beneath the Watchtower. Next to it is a gate sealed with a large chain, which you can break with the Cleave Axe. On the other side, you'll meet Wan Jun, The Coppermaul.

If you've got decision paralysis about which Phantom Edges to use, look no further.

Once you open the gate and face the behemoth that is Wan Jun, you'll have to protect yourself first and foremost. He deals less damage than his size might suggest, but luckily moves just as slowly as you might think. He's also clumsy, which opens the door for strikes that can whittle down his health and guard.

Most of his attacks will involve the Bashpole and can be parried or dodged. Because he's so slow, dodging is easier than with some other bosses, letting you build as much distance as you need. He can't close the gap quickly, so you have time to react.

Don't be afraid to get in a few quick strikes while he collects himself. He's slow and remains open for a while.

Watch out for double strikes with the Bashpole, as those are his fastest attacks. Unlike some bosses with heavy armor or shields like Wu Xuan, The Fallen General, you can break his guard with attacks, so you don't have to parry or dodge to break it. Stay patient as he goes through his attacks, and use the openings to strike when he falls over or stumbles.

Using the Bashpole is pretty simple, given that it's really just a giant metal melon on a stick. When equipped, you can hold your Phantom Edge button (R2 on PS5) to swing the Bashpole around your head three times, then slam it down onto the ground. If you release the button early, you'll slam it down before completing three rotations.

For traversal, you don't need to equip the Bashpole, just like the other traversal Phantom Edges. When you're near a weak floor made out of wooden boards, you can press R2 to slam the Bashpole into the ground, causing Soul to fall through into the level below.

Which skills will get you the farthest through the Wulin?

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/payers/medicare-part-d-premiums-projected-rise-50-2027-without-plan-switching-kff" hreflang="en">Medicare Part D premiums projected to rise 50% in 2027 without plan switching: KFF</a>

📅 2026-11-03  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
Medicare Part D premiums projected to rise 50% in 2027 without plan switching: KFF" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Medicare beneficiaries who remain in their current stand-alone Part D drug plans could face sharply higher premiums in 2027 if they don’t switch to lower-cost plans during open enrollment, according to a KFF analysis.

The average monthly premium for stand-alone prescription drug plans, or PDPs, is projected to rise 50%, from $34 in 2026 to $51 in 2027, if current enrollees remain in the same plans, according to the analysis from KFF, an independent source for health policy research, polling and news.

The increase would widen the gap between stand-alone drug plans and Medicare Advantage plans that include prescription drug coverage. KFF projects that drug coverage through Medicare Advantage plans that offer Part D coverage, or MA-PDs, will average $9 a month in 2027, compared with $51 for PDPs. The average PDP premium would rise by $17, compared with an increase of less than $2 for MA-PDs.

For Medicare Part D prescription drug coverage, the average beneficiary has 28 Medicare Advantage prescription drug plans (MA-PDs) and 9 stand-alone prescription drug plans (PDPs) to choose from, with varying costs associated with these different options. KFF analysts examined premiums and cost-sharing requirements for Part D coverage in 2027 and how these costs have changed over time, based on Medicare plan landscape and benefits data from the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS).

The $51 figure assumes current PDP enrollees remain in their existing plans. The actual average premium could be lower as beneficiaries switch to less expensive plans during open enrollment and new beneficiaries select coverage.

CMS projected a much smaller increase in the national average Part D premium, from $35 to $36. KFF noted that CMS, which based its estimate on September 2026 enrollment, did not explain its calculation methodology.

Among PDP enrollees who do not receive low-income subsidies, 76%, or 10.4 million people, would pay higher premiums if they keep their current plans. About 2.4 million would see their monthly premiums increase by more than $50.

That's a sharp increase from 2026, when only 38,000 enrollees faced monthly premium increases exceeding $50, according to KFF. The Trump administration’s decision to discontinue the Part D Premium Stabilization Demonstration after 2026 ended the additional premium subsidies that were provided to PDP sponsors and the cap on the maximum allowable monthly PDP premium increase, which was set at $50 for 2026, according to KFF analysts.

Another 4.1 million beneficiaries without low-income subsidies are enrolled in zero-premium PDPs this year, but no zero-premium PDPs will be available in 2027.

“With some plans increasing premiums substantially, the stakes are higher this year for people with Medicare stand-alone drug coverage if they sit on the sidelines during open enrollment,” Juliette Cubanski, a vice president and director of the Program on Medicare Policy at KFF, said in a statement. “There are relatively low-premium PDP options, but people facing large premium increases need to take action if they want to avoid paying significantly more.”

According to KFF's analysis, if PDP enrollees who are not receiving Part D Low-Income Subsidies (LIS) keep their current plan in 2027, more than half (55%), or 7.5 million people, will pay premiums of less than $20 per month, but nearly 30%, or 3.9 million people, will pay at least $100 per month.

Average monthly premiums for the eight national PDPs are projected to range from around $6 to $204 in 2027. Among national PDPs, the two lowest-premium plans are Centene’s Wellcare Value Script Rx, an enhanced PDP with an average premium of $6.50 for 2027, up from $5.80 in 2026,  and Wellcare Classic, a basic PDP with an average premium of $10.90 for 2027, up from $8.60 in 2026, according to the analysis. The national PDPs with the highest average monthly premiums are United’s AARP Medicare Rx Preferred at $204.50, up from $126.00 in 2026, and Humana Premier Rx Plan at $137.40, up from $119.60 in 2026.

The analysis also finds that most Part D enrollees will face a deductible of several hundred dollars for drug coverage in 2027, including 85% of MA-PD enrollees, up from 80% in 2026, and virtually all PDP enrollees, 99.8%, up from 96% in 2026, based on current enrollment. The average Part D deductible in 2027 will be $643 for PDP enrollees, up from $540 in 2026,  and $489 for MA-PD enrollees, rising from $360 in 2026. The standard Part D deductible in 2027 is $700, up from $615 in 2026.

Most Part D enrollees will also face coinsurance rather than fixed copayments for preferred-brand and non-preferred drugs, potentially making their out-of-pocket costs less predictable. Virtually all PDP enrollees will face median coinsurance of 25% for preferred brands and 35% for non-preferred drugs, according to KFF.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/payers/humana-athenahealth-collaborate-expanded-electronic-prior-authorizations-nationwide" hreflang="en">Humana, athenahealth collaborate on expanded electronic prior authorizations nationwide</a>

📅 2026-11-03  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
Humana, athenahealth collaborate on expanded electronic prior authorizations nationwide" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Humana and athenahealth are expanding electronic prior authorizations nationwide in a newly announced collaboration.

Through athenaPayer, Humana is able to connect with over 180,000 providers across the athenaOne network, which the partners said will create a more connected experience between the health plan and clinicians.

“Every phone call, fax and portal log-in adds work for a practice trying to get a patient the care they need,” said Paul Brient, athenahealth chief product and operations officer, in a statement. “Prior authorization shouldn’t require care teams to navigate a different process for every health plan. By connecting health plans and practices through one solution with a common workflow, we’re creating a more scalable way to exchange information, reducing work for both providers and payers while, most importantly, helping patients access care faster.”

Athenahealth internal data show more than 6,000 providers processed nearly 35,000 electronic prior authorizations with Humana in September, with over 70% completed without requiring additional information from staff.

The collaboration reflects Humana’s broader strategy to improve care access through requiring fewer prior authorizations and making the process simpler when needed, executives said.

“Our goal is straightforward: help people get the right care at the right time while reducing unnecessary burden on the physicians and care teams who serve them,” said Traci Granston, M.D., Humana vice president of clinical innovation and trend, in a statement. “… This collaboration with athenahealth is another step forward, ensuring that providers spend less time navigating administrative processes and more time focused on patient care.”

In June 2025, insurers nationwide—including Humana—agreed to take steps to reform prior authorization, including supporting electronic prior authorizations.
